Tapes as Stochastic Matrices of String Diagrams
Filippo Bonchi, Cipriano Junior Cioffo

Abstract
Tape diagrams provide a graphical notation for categories equipped with two monoidal products, ⊗ \otimes and ⊕ \oplus , where ⊕ \oplus is a biproduct. Recently, they have been generalised to handle Kleisli categories of arbitrary monoidal monads. In this work, we show that for the subdistribution monad, tapes are isomorphic to stochastic matrices of subdistributions of string diagrams. We then exploit this result to provide a complete axiomatisation of probabilistic Boolean circuits.



1
Introduction

Driven by the growing interest in compositional semantics for probabilistic systems, an increasing body of work
[
10
,
27
,
40
,
5
,
15
,
14
,
28
,
37
,
20
,
19
,
22
,
44
,
39
,
21
,
18
]
employs
string diagrams

[
30
,
42
]
, which formally represent morphisms in a strict symmetric monoidal category freely generated by a monoidal signature. These diagrams are typically interpreted in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
, the Kleisli category of the subdistribution monad
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}
(or suitable variants), with the monoidal structure induced by the cartesian product
\otimes
.
Much research has focused on the role of the
copier

and the
discharger

, and their correspondence to marginals and joint distributions.

In contrast, significantly less attention
[
17
]
has been devoted to the second monoidal tensor
\oplus
in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
, which corresponds to disjoint union of sets, even though its interaction with
\otimes
plays a pivotal role in many probabilistic frameworks
[
44
,
11
,
33
]
. One possible reason is that standard string diagrams are inherently limited to representing a single monoidal structure at a time. To overcome this limitation, we focus on
tape diagrams

[
4
]
, which can be informally understood as string diagrams of string diagrams. This more expressive graphical language supports simultaneous reasoning about both
\otimes
and
\oplus
, in particular in categories where
\oplus
forms a
biproduct
, i.e., serves as both categorical product and coproduct. Moreover, as recently shown
[
2
]
, tape diagrams extend naturally to Kleisli categories of arbitrary monoidal monads and thus, in particular, to
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
, which is the main focus of this work.

Our starting observation is that the coproduct
\oplus
in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
enjoys an additional universal property, akin to that of a product, which makes it resemble a biproduct. We refer to categories where
\oplus
satisfies this property as
convex biproduct categories
(Definition
2
), and we study their associated monoidal algebras. While categories with finite biproducts are equipped with natural monoids and
comonoids
, convex biproduct categories carry natural monoids and
co-pointed convex algebras
, which are the dual of pointed convex algebras (pca)–the Eilenberg-Moore algebras for the subdistribution monad
[
45
,
6
,
7
,
36
]
.

There is a further striking analogy with the theory of finite biproduct categories: given any category
\mathbf{C}
enriched over monoids, one can construct the category
\mathbf{Mat}(\mathbf{C})
of matrices over
\mathbf{C}

[
34
]
, which possesses finite biproducts. Analogously, starting from a category
\mathbf{C}
enriched over pcas, one can define the convex biproduct category
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
of
stochastic matrices
over
\mathbf{C}
(Proposition
3
). Composition is defined via matrix multiplication: sums are given by the convex sums of the pca-enrichment and products by composition of arrows in
\mathbf{C}
.
We show that this construction yields a functor
\mathbf{StMat}(-)
from the category
\mathbf{PCACat}
of pca-enriched categories to the category
\mathbf{CBCat}
of convex biproduct categories, which is left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U
(Theorem
4.1
):

\textstyle{\mathbf{Cat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{+}}
\textstyle{\;\;{\tiny{\bot}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{PCACat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{StMat}(-)}
\textstyle{\;{\tiny{\bot}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CBCat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}

The leftmost adjunction is standard (see, e.g.,
[
8
,
46
]
): the functor
(-)^{+}
freely enriches a locally small category
\mathbf{C}
over pcas. The resulting category
\mathbf{C}^{+}
has arrows given by subdistributions of arrows in
\mathbf{C}
.

Our key result is that the composite of the two adjunctions admits a syntactic presentation in terms of generators and equations. Specifically, we define a functor
\mathbf{T}(-)\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
that freely adds to each category
\mathbf{C}
a monoidal structure with natural monoids, and co-pointed convex algebras. We show that
\mathbf{T}(-)
is left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{CBCat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
(Theorem
5.2
) and we thus conclude that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}^{+})
(Corollary
1
).

Importantly, when
\mathbf{C}
is a category of string diagrams
\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
,
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})
coincides with the category of tape diagrams for the monad
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}
introduced in
[
2
]
. A result from
[
2
]
shows that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})
admits two monoidal products,
\oplus
and
\otimes
, forming a
rig category
(also known as a bimonoidal category)
[
32
,
29
]
. The isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}^{+})
then provides a concrete interpretation of tapes as stochastic matrices whose entries are probability distributions of string diagrams. Interestingly,
\oplus
is direct sum of matrices and
\otimes
is (an extended) Kronecker product.

We argue that this isomorphism can be fruitfully applied to give more principled axiomatisations of probabilistic languages based on string diagrams, and to streamline their completeness proofs. As a case study, we revisit the complete axiomatisation of
probabilistic Boolean circuits
from
[
40
]
. These can be encoded into tape diagrams of Boolean circuits resulting in a more effective realisation of probabilistic control (see
[
2
, Example 30]
). We show that (Corollary
3
) adding a single extra axiom–originally appearing in
[
13
]
– to the axioms of Boolean circuits and the tape structure (i.e., natural monoids and co-pcas) suffices to derive an alternative complete axiomatisation. The proof of completeness amounts to several categorical observations, two almost trivial lemmas and the universal properties of convex biproducts.

Synopsis.
We begin in Section
2
by recalling the notions of pointed convex algebras, pca-enriched categories, and the leftmost adjunction in the diagram above. Section
3
introduces convex biproduct categories and their associated monoidal algebraic structures. In Section
4
, we define stochastic matrices and establish the rightmost adjunction. The syntactic construction
\mathbf{T}(-)
, corresponding to the composite adjunction, is presented in Section
5
. This construction is then applied to categories of string diagrams in Section
6
to obtain tape diagrams. Finally, Section
7
revisits probabilistic Boolean circuits from
[
40
]
, explains their encoding as probabilistic tapes from
[
2
]
, and presents the alternative axiomatisation along with the completeness proof.

2
Pointed Convex Algebras

We commence our exposition by recalling several well known algebraic structures.

A
pointed convex algebra
(pca)
[
45
]
, consists of a set
X
, a designed element
\star\in X
and, for all
p
in the open real interval
(0,1)
, a function
+_{p}\colon X\times X\to X
such that, by fixing
\tilde{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}pq
and
\tilde{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq}
, the following laws hold for all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in X
.

(x_{1}+_{q}x_{2})+_{p}x_{3}=x_{1}+_{\tilde{p}}(x_{2}+_{\tilde{q}}x_{3})\qquad x_{1}+_{p}x_{2}=x_{2}+_{1-p}x_{1}\qquad x_{1}+_{p}x_{1}=x_{1}

(1)

A morphism of pcas is a function preserving
\star
and
+_{p}
. We denote by
\mathbf{PCA}
the category of PCAs and their morphisms.

In any pca,
+_{p}
can be defined for all
p\in[0,1]
by setting
x+_{1}y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}x
and
x+_{0}y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}y
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in[0,1]
such that
\sum_{i}p_{i}\leq 1
, one defines
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\cdot(-)_{i}\colon X^{n}\to X
inductively as

\sum_{i=1}^{0}p_{i}\cdot(-)_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\star\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}p_{i}\cdot(-)_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(-)_{1}+_{p_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}\cdot(-)_{j}\text{.}

(2)

where
(-)_{j}=(-)_{i+1}
and
q_{j}
is
\frac{p_{i+1}}{1-p_{1}}
if
p_{1}\neq 1
and
0
otherwise.
Note that when
n=1
,
\sum_{i=1}^{1}p_{i}\cdot(-)_{i}
is, by definition,
(-)_{1}+_{p_{1}}\star
. Since this operation will play a crucial role we name it
multiplication by a scalar

p\in[0,1]
and we fix the following notation.

p\cdot x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}x+_{p}\star

The standard example of pca is provided by the set
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(X)
of finitely supported probability subdistributions over some set
X
. Recall that a finitely supported subdistribution over
X
is a function
d\colon X\to[0,1]
such that
\sum_{x\in X}d(x)\leq 1
and
d(x)\neq 0
for finitely many
x
. The designed element
\star
in
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(X)
is the null subdistribution, i.e.,
\star(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}0
for all
x\in X
; given
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(X)
, for all
p\in(0,1)
,
(d_{1}+_{p}d_{2})(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}p\cdot d_{1}(x)+(1-p)\cdot d_{2}(x)
. At this point, it is convenient to fix some extra notation:
\mathcal{D}(X)
is the set of all finitely supported distributions (i.e.,
\sum_{x\in X}d(x)=1
) and, for all
x\in X
,
\delta_{x}
is the Dirac distribution at
x
(i.e.,
\delta_{x}(x^{\prime})=1
if
x=x^{\prime}
and
0
otherwise).

A category
\mathbf{C}
is
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched
if every homset
\mathbf{C}[X,Y]
carries a pca and composition of arrows is a pcas morphism, i.e., for all
p\in(0,1)
and properly typed arrows
e,f,g,h
, the followings hold.

e;(f+_{p}g)=(e;f)+_{p}(e;g)\qquad(f+_{p}g);h=(f;h+_{p}g;h)\qquad f;\star=\star=\star;f

(3)

A functor
F
is
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched if it preserves the structure of pca over each homset.
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched categories and functors form a category, denoted by
\mathbf{PCACat}
.
All categories considered hereafter are tacitly assumed to be locally small:
\mathbf{Cat}
stands for the category of locally small categories. Note that there is a forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{PCACat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Any category
\mathbf{C}
, gives rise to the
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
\mathbf{C}^{+}
: objects of
\mathbf{C}^{+}
are those of
\mathbf{C}
; for all objects
X,Y
, the homset is defined as
\mathbf{C}^{+}[X,Y]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{C}[X,Y])
. For
d_{1}\colon X\to Y
and
d_{2}\colon Y\to Z
, their composition
d_{1};d_{2}\colon X\to Z
is defined for all
h\in\mathbf{C}[X,Z]
as
d_{1};d_{2}(h)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\{(f,g)\mid f;g=h\}}d_{1}(f)\cdot d_{2}(g)
; The identity
{id}_{X}\colon X\to X
is given by
\delta_{id_{X}}
. One can easily see that
\mathbf{C}^{+}
is a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category. Moreover, the assignment
\mathbf{C}\mapsto\mathbf{C}^{+}
gives rise to a functor
(-)^{+}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{PCACat}
which is left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U
(see e.g.
[
8
, Prop. 6.4.7]
or
[
46
, Cor. 1]
).

Theorem 2.1
(
[
8
,
46
]
)

(-)^{+}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{PCACat}
is left adjoint to
U\colon\mathbf{PCACat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Example 1

Let
\mathbf{1}
be a category with a single object and a single arrow. The
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
\mathbf{1}^{+}
has a single object, arrows are
p\in[0,1]
and composition is given by multiplication.

For a set
A
, the set of words over
A
(hereafter denoted by
A^{*}
) carries the structure of a category with a single object. The
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
(A^{*})^{+}
has a single object and arrows are subdistributions over
A^{*}
. Composition of
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(A^{*})
is defined for all words
w\in A^{*}
as
d_{1};d_{2}(w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{u;v=w}d_{1}(u)\cdot d_{2}(v)
.

Our main example of
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category is
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
, the Kleisli category of the monad
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}\colon\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
(see e.g.,
[
24
]
).
Objects are sets; morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
are functions
X\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(Y)
. We often write
f(y\mid x)
for
f(x)(y)
as this number represents the probability that
f
returns
y
given the input
x
.
Identities
{id}_{X}\colon X\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(X)
map each element
x\in X
to
\delta_{x}
.
For two functions
f\colon X\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(Y)
and
g\colon Y\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(Z)
, their composition in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
is defined as
f;g(z\mid x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{y\in Y}f(y\mid x)\cdot g(z\mid y)
.

In our work, it is fundamental that
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
carries two symmetric monoidal structures:
(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq}),\otimes,1)
and
(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq}),\oplus,0)
. The monoidal product
\otimes
is defined on objects as the cartesian product of sets, often denoted by
\times
, with unit the singleton
1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{\bullet\}
;
\oplus
is the disjoint union of sets with unit the empty set
0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{\}
. Hereafter we denote the disjoint union of two sets
X
and
Y
by
X\oplus Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{(x,0)\mid x\in X\}\cup\{(y,1)\mid y\in Y\}
where
0
and
1
are tags used to distinguish the set of provenance. For arrows
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
,
f\otimes g\colon X\otimes X^{\prime}\to Y\otimes Y^{\prime}
and
f\oplus g\colon X\oplus X^{\prime}\to Y\oplus Y^{\prime}
are defined, for all
x\in X
,
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
,
y\in Y
,
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
,
u\in X\oplus X^{\prime}
and
v\in Y\oplus Y^{\prime}
, as follows.

\displaystyle f\otimes g(y,y^{\prime}\mid x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}f(y\mid x)\cdot g(y^{\prime}\mid x^{\prime})

(4)

\displaystyle f\oplus g(v\mid u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}f(y\mid x)&\text{if }u=(x,0)\text{ and }v=(y,0)\\
g(y^{\prime}\mid x^{\prime})&\text{if }u=(x^{\prime},1)\text{ and }v=(y^{\prime},1)\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Symmetries
\sigma^{\otimes}_{X,Y}\colon X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X
and
\sigma^{\oplus}_{X,Y}\colon X\oplus Y\to Y\oplus X
are defined as in
\mathbf{Set}
. Actually,
(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq}),\oplus,\otimes,0,1)
forms a rig (aka bimonoidal) category in the sense of
[
32
]
. We will come back to rig categories in Section
6
. Until then, we will focus only on the monoidal structure provided by
\oplus
.

3
Convex Biproduct Categories

As is the case for all Kleisli categories of monads on
\mathbf{Set}
, the category
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
inherits coproducts from
\mathbf{Set}
: the operation
\oplus
in (
4
), serves as a coproduct in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
. Our initial observation is that
\oplus
satisfies an additional universal property, which we refer to as the
convex product
, described below.

Definition 1

Let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
be two objects of a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
\mathbf{C}
. The
convex product
of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
is an object
Z
with two arrows
\pi_{1}\colon Z\to X_{1}
and
\pi_{2}\colon Z\to X_{2}
satisfying the following property: for all
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
p_{1}+p_{2}\leq 1
and all arrows
f\colon A\to X_{1}
,
g\colon A\to X_{2}
, there exists a unique arrow
h\colon A\to Z
such that
h;\pi_{1}=p_{1}\cdot f
and
h;\pi_{2}=p_{2}\cdot g
.

Similarly, the convex product of
n
objects
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
is an object
Z
with arrows
\pi_{i}\colon Z\to X_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
satisfying the following property: for all
p_{1},\dots p_{n}\in[0,1]
where
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\leq 1
and arrows
f_{i}\colon A\to X_{i}
, there exists a unique arrow
h\colon A\to Z
such that
h;\pi_{i}=p_{i}\cdot f_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. Observe that, by definition, the 0-ary convex product is a final object. Hereafter, we will denote the unique arrow
h
by
\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\rangle_{\vec{p}}
where
\vec{p}
is a compact notation for
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
.

Definition 2

A
convex biproduct category
is a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
\mathbf{C}
with an object
0
which is both initial and final and, for every pair of objects
X_{1},X_{2}
, an object
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
and morphisms
\pi_{i}\colon X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\to X_{i}
and
\iota_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
such that
(X_{1}\oplus X_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2})
is a coproduct,
(X_{1}\oplus X_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is a convex product and

\iota_{i};\pi_{j}=\delta_{i,j}

(5)

where
\delta_{i,j}\colon X_{i}\to X_{j}
is defined as
\delta_{i,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{X_{i}}
if
i=j
and
\delta_{i,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\star_{X_{i},X_{j}}
otherwise. A morphism of convex biproduct categories is a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
preserving finite coproducts. We write
\mathbf{CBCat}
for the category of convex biproduct categories and their morphisms.

Lemma 1

In a convex biproduct category
\mathbf{C}
, the enrichment is compatible with respect to the (convex-co)products, namely,

f+_{p}g=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[{id}_{Y},{id}_{Y}]

(6)

for all
f,g\colon X\to Y
and
p\in[0,1]
.

Proof

Consider the arrows
f;\iota_{1}
and
g;\iota_{2}
from
X
to
Y\oplus Y
and their sum
f;\iota_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2}
. The following computation shows that

\displaystyle(f;\iota_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2});\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=f,\iota_{1};\pi_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2};\pi_{1}

(pca-enrichment)

\displaystyle=f+_{p}\star_{X,Y}

(by (
5
))

\displaystyle=p\cdot f

and similarly
(f;\iota_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2});\pi_{2}=(1-p)\cdot g
. By the universal property of convex products, we conclude that
f;\iota_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2}=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p}
. Composing both sides with
[{id}_{Y},{id}_{Y}]
we obtain

\displaystyle f+_{p}g

\displaystyle=(f;\iota_{1}+_{p}g;\iota_{2});[{id}_{Y},{id}_{Y}]

(pca-enrichment)

\displaystyle=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[{id}_{Y},{id}_{Y}].

As for categories with finite biproducts, morphisms of convex biproduct categories preserve (convex) products (see Proposition
9
in Appendix
0.D
).

Lemma 2

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a convex biproduct category. Then
\mathbf{C}
has
n
-ary convex products.

Since any convex biproduct category
\mathbf{C}
has finite coproducts, by Fox theorem
[
16
]
, it carries a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbf{C},\oplus,0)
where every object
X
is equipped with a commutative monoid that is natural and coherent, i.e., morphisms
\rhd_{X}\colon X\oplus X\to X
and
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}\colon 0\to X
satisfying the laws in Figures
4
and
5
where
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
,
\lambda_{X}
,
\rho_{Y}
denote associators, left and right unitors. Recall that
\rhd_{X}
is given as the copairing of the identities
[{id}_{X},{id}_{X}]
while
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}
is the unique arrow from the initial object.

Similarly, the convex product structure equips any object
X
with a natural and coherent
co-pca
: for all
p\in(0,1)
, there exist an arrow
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}\colon X\to X\oplus X
, defined as
\langle id_{X},id_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p}
, and an arrow
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X}\colon X\to 0
, defined as the unique map to the final object
0
, satisfying the laws in Figures
6
and
7
.

Proposition 1

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a convex biproduct category. Then for every object
X
, the triple
(X,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X},\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X})
is a natural and coherent co-pca, i.e., the axioms in Figures
6
and
7
hold.

In Appendix
0.B
, we illustrate some examples of convex biproduct categories, such as
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
and its continuous analogue. The most relevant for our work is
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
.
For all sets
X
, co-pcas and monoids in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
are illustrated below.

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\begin{array}[]{rcl}{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}\colon X&\to&X\oplus X\\
x&\mapsto&\delta_{(x,0)}+_{p}\delta_{(x,1)}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X}\colon X&\to&0\\
x&\mapsto&\star\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}\colon 0&\to&X\\
\text{ }\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rhd_{X}\colon X\oplus X&\to&X\\
(x,i)&\mapsto&\delta_{x}\end{array}\end{array}

(7)

Monoids and co-pcas will be useful later to freely generate convex biproduct categories. In particular, morphisms of convex biproduct categories can be characterised as follows.

Proposition 2

A functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
is a morphism of convex biproduct categories if and only if it is a strong monoidal functor preserving monoids and co-pcas.

The axioms in Figures
4
,
5
,
6
, and
7

4
Stochastic Matrices over PCA-enriched categories

It is well known (see, e.g.,
[
34
, Exercises VIII.2.5-6]
) that from a category enriched over commutative monoids, one can freely generate the finite biproduct category of its matrices. In this section, we show that a similar construction holds for
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched categories: given such a category
\mathbf{C}
, one can construct the convex biproduct category
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
of
stochastic matrices
over
\mathbf{C}
.

Objects of
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
are words in
Ob(\mathbf{C})^{*}
. We will write
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}U_{k}
for the word
U_{1}\dots U_{n}
and
0
for the empty word. We will denote objects of
\mathbf{C}
by
U,V
and those of
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
by
P,Q
. In
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
, an arrow
M\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}
is an equivalence class of
m\times n
matrices with
(j,i)
-entries given by pairs
(p_{ji},f_{ji})
where
f_{ji}\in\mathbf{C}[U_{i},V_{j}]
and
p_{ji}\in[0,1]
satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ji}\leq 1
. Two matrices
M
and
M^{\prime}
are equivalent, in symbols
M\equiv M^{\prime}
, if
p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}=p^{\prime}_{ji}\cdot f^{\prime}_{ji}
in
\mathbf{C}[U_{i},V_{j}]
for all
i,j
.

Remark 1

The use of pairs
(p_{ji},f_{ji})
as entries of matrices is necessary to specify the constraints
\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ji}\leq 1
. However,
\equiv
ensures that one can safely write
M_{ji}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}
.

The composition of two morphisms
M\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}
and
M^{\prime}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{l}W_{k}
is obtained by matrix multiplication
M^{\prime}M
: for all
u\in\{1,\dots,l\}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
the entry at
(u,i)
is

(M^{\prime}M)_{ui}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}r_{ui}\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{p^{\prime}_{uj}p_{ji}}{r_{ui}}\cdot(f_{ji};f^{\prime}_{uj})\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{uj}p_{ji}\cdot(f_{ji};f^{\prime}_{uj})

(8)

where the convex sums
\sum_{k}p_{k}\cdot(-)_{k}
are those provided by the
\mathbf{PCA}
enrichment of
\mathbf{C}
, the composition
;
is in
\mathbf{C}
and

r_{ui}=(\sum_{j=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{uj}p_{ji})
. Simple computations confirm that
\sum_{u=1}^{l}r_{ui}\leq 1
,
\frac{p^{\prime}_{uj}p_{ji}}{r_{ui}}\in[0,1]
and that the equality in (
8
) follows from Lemma
7
.
8
in Appendix
0.C
.
For all objects
P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
,
{id}_{P}
is the
n\times n
matrix with entries
({id}_{P})_{jj}=1\cdot{id}_{U_{j}}
and, for
i\neq j
,
({id}_{P})_{ji}=0\cdot\star
.

Example 2

Recall
\mathbf{1}^{+}
and
(A^{*})^{+}
from Example
1
.
In
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{1}^{+})
objects are natural numbers and arrows
n\to m
are the usual
m\times n
sub-stochastic matrices (i.e.,
\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ji}\leq 1
).
In
\mathbf{StMat}((A^{*})^{+})
objects are natural numbers and arrows
n\to m
are
m\times n
matrices with entries in
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(A^{*})
. Consider for instance the matrices
N\colon 2\to 3
and
M\colon 2\to 2
on the left below. The composition
M;N\colon 2\to 3
is the matrix on the right.

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\cdot a&\frac{1}{2}\cdot c\\
\frac{1}{3}\cdot ab&0\cdot\star\\
0\cdot\star&\frac{1}{3}\cdot{id}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\cdot a&1\cdot c\\
\frac{1}{2}\cdot ab&0\cdot\star\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\frac{1}{2}\cdot aa+\frac{1}{2}abc)&\frac{1}{2}\cdot ca\\
\frac{1}{6}\cdot aab&\frac{1}{3}\cdot cab\\
\frac{1}{6}\cdot ab&0\cdot\star\end{pmatrix}

Proposition 3

\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is a convex biproduct category.

For arbitrary matrices
M
and
N
,
M\oplus N
is defined as below on the left

M\oplus N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}M&\emptyset\\
\emptyset&N\end{pmatrix}\qquad\sigma_{U,Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}\emptyset&{id}_{Q}\\
{id}_{P}&\emptyset\end{pmatrix}

(9)

where
\emptyset
is the matrix with all entries
0\cdot\star
. The symmetry
\sigma^{\oplus}_{P,Q}\colon P\oplus Q\to Q\oplus P
is defined as on the right above. Every object
P
is equipped with co-pca and monoids. These are defined for all objects
U\in\mathbf{C}
as follows, where
\mathexclamationmark_{U}
(respectively
?_{U}
) is the unique matrix with
0
rows (columns).

^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}p\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-p)\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathexclamationmark_{U}\qquad\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}?_{U}\qquad\rhd_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}1\cdot{id}_{U}&1\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}

(10)

For arbitrary objects
P
of
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
, co-pca and monoids are defined inductively as:

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{array}[]{ccc}{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}&\;\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}&\;\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U\oplus P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{ccc}\rhd_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}&\;\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}&\;\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U\oplus P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P}\end{array}\\
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U\oplus P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}\oplus\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P});({id}_{U}\oplus\sigma_{U,P}\oplus{id}_{P})&\rhd_{U\oplus P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}({id}_{U}\oplus\sigma_{P,U}\oplus{id}_{P});(\rhd_{U}\oplus\rhd_{P})\end{array}

(11)

Like categories of matrices are freely generated finite biproduct categories
[
34
]
, similarly
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is the convex biproduct category freely generated by
\mathbf{C}
. We are going to illustrate this below.

First, for all
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched functors
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
, one can define
\mathbf{StMat}(F)\colon\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{D})
as the functor mapping an object
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
to
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}F(U_{k})
and a matrix

M\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}
with entries
M_{ji}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}
into the matrix with entries
\mathbf{StMat}(F)(M)_{ji}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}p_{ji}\cdot F(f_{ji})
.

Proposition 4

Let
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
be a PCA-enriched functor.
\mathbf{StMat}(F)\colon\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{D})
is a morphism of convex biproduct categories.

Then, it is easy to check that the assignment
\mathbf{C}\mapsto\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
and
F\mapsto\mathbf{StMat}(F)
provides a functor
\mathbf{StMat}(-):\mathbf{PCACat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
which is left adjoint to the forgetful
U\colon\mathbf{CBCat}\to\mathbf{PCACat}
.

Theorem 4.1

\mathbf{StMat}(-)\colon\mathbf{PCACat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
is left adjoint to
U\colon\mathbf{CBCat}\to\mathbf{PCACat}

We conclude this section with a simple observation that will be useful later in Section
7
.

Proposition 5

For any convex bicategory
\mathbf{C}
, the counit of the adjunction above

\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}\colon\mathbf{StMat}(U(\mathbf{C}))\to\mathbf{C}
is a full and faithful morphism of convex bicategories.

5
An equational presentation of stochastic matrices

\begin{array}[]{c}\hline\cr\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}\colon U\to U\oplus U}&{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\colon U\to 0}&{\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}\colon U\oplus V\to V\oplus U}&{\rhd_{U}\colon U\oplus U\to U}&{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}\colon 0\to U}\end{array}\\[3.00003pt]
\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{id_{0}\colon 0\to 0}&{id_{U}\colon U\to U}&\frac{c\colon U\to V}{\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}\colon U\to V}&\frac{\mathfrak{t}\colon P\to Q\mathfrak{s}\colon Q\to R}{\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{s}\colon P\to R}&\frac{\mathfrak{t}\colon P_{1}\to Q_{1}\mathfrak{s}\colon P_{2}\to Q_{2}}{\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{s}\colon P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[5.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{cc}\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{c}(f;g);h=f;(g;h)\qquad id_{P};f=f=f;id_{Q}\\
(f_{1}\oplus f_{2});(g_{1}\oplus g_{2})=(f_{1};g_{1})\oplus(f_{2};g_{2})\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}id_{0}\oplus f=f=f\oplus id_{0}\qquad(f\oplus g)\,\oplus h=f\oplus\,(g\oplus h)\\
\sigma_{P,Q};\sigma_{Q,P}=id_{P\oplus Q}\qquad(s\oplus id_{R});\sigma_{Q,R}=\sigma_{P,R};(id_{R}\oplus s)\end{array}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 3
:
Typing rules (top) and axioms (bottom) for freely generated strict symmetric monoidal categories.

So far, we have seen that given a category, one can first freely enrich it over
\mathbf{PCA}
, and then obtain a convex biproduct category by taking its category of stochastic matrices.
Now, we give a syntactic description, in terms of generators and equations, of such category.

For a category
\mathbf{C}
, we consider terms generated by the following grammar

\begin{array}[]{rcccccccccccccccccccc}\mathfrak{t}&::=&{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}&\mid&\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}&\mid&\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}&\mid&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}&\mid&\rhd_{U}&\mid&{id}_{U}&\mid&{id}_{0}&\mid&\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}&\mid&\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{t}&\mid&\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{t}\end{array}

(12)

where
p\in(0,1)
,
U,V\in Ob(\mathbf{C})
, and
c
is an arrow in
\mathbf{C}
. Terms are typed according to the rules at the top of Table
3
: each type is an arrow
P\to Q
where
P,Q\in Ob(\mathbf{C})^{*}
are regarded as sums of objects of
\mathbf{C}
. As expected, we consider only those terms that are typable. For arbitrary
P\in Ob(\mathbf{C})^{*}
, we define

{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P},\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P},\rhd_{P}
as in (
11
). Analogous inductive definitions give us
{id}_{P}
and
\sigma^{\oplus}_{P,Q}
.

The category
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has as set of objects
Ob(\mathbf{C})^{*}
. Arrows in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})[P,Q]
are terms modulo the axioms of natural and coherent monoids, co-pcas, strict symmetric monoidal categories (respectively in Tables
1
,
2
and
3
bottom) and the following two axioms.

\underline{\smash{\overline{\;{id}_{P}\vphantom{t}\;}}}={id}_{P}\qquad\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c;d\vphantom{t}\;}}}=\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}};\underline{\smash{\overline{\;d\vphantom{t}\;}}}

(Tape)

Identities, composition, symmetries and monoidal product are defined as on terms. It is thus immediate to see that
(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C}),\oplus,0)
forms a symmetric monoidal category. Moreover such category is enriched over
\mathbf{PCA}
: for all objects
P,Q
and arrows
f,g\colon P\to Q

f+_{p}g\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\ ^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P};(f\oplus g);\rhd_{Q}\qquad\star_{P,Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}

(13)

By naturality of

\multimap

and

\multimapinv

,
0
is both initial and final object. By the Fox theorem
[
16
]
,
\oplus
is a coproduct with injections
{id}_{P_{1}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
and
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P_{1}}\oplus{id}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{2}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
.

Our main technical effort consists in proving that
\oplus
is a convex product with projections
{id}_{P_{1}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{1}
and
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P_{1}}\oplus{id}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{2}
. By the coproduct property any
\mathfrak{t}\colon\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}V_{j}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
is the copairing
[\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{m}]
for
\mathfrak{t}_{j}=\iota_{j};\mathfrak{t}
. One can thus restrict to consider the case of arrow of type
V_{j}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
.
First, we extend
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
to arbitrary
p\in[0,1]
:
{}^{0\;\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}\oplus{id}_{U}
and
{}^{1\;\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{U}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}
. Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
with
p_{i}\in[0,1]
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\leq 1
, we inductively define
{}^{\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U
as follows

^{\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\qquad^{\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\,^{\;p_{1}}\!\!\!\lhd_{U};({id}_{U}\oplus^{\;\vec{q}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n})

(14)

where
\vec{q}=q_{1},\dots q_{n}
for
q_{i}=0
if
p_{1}=1
and
q_{i}=\frac{p_{i+1}}{1-p_{1}}
otherwise.
Given
n
arrows
t_{i}\colon U\to U_{i}
, simple computations confirm that
(^{\;\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i});\pi_{i}=p_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{i}
. Thus,
{}^{\;\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
is a mediating arrow satisfying the constraints of
n
-ary convex products. The hard part consists in proving its uniqueness. We rely on two key properties of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
:

Lemma 3

For all
\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
, there exist
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
and
t_{i}\colon U\to U_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\,^{\;\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i}
.

Lemma 4

[Cancellativity]
For all
r\in(0,1)
, for all
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}
,
if
r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}
then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Theorem 5.1

\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is a convex biproduct category. In particular, for all
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon U\to U_{i}
,
\langle\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots\mathfrak{t}_{n}\rangle_{\vec{p}}=^{\;\;\vec{p}}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i}
.

The assignment
\mathbf{C}\mapsto\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
gives rise to a functor
\mathbf{T}(-)\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
which is left adjoint to the forgetfull functor
U\colon\mathbf{CBCat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Theorem 5.2

\mathbf{T}(-)\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
is left adjoint to
U\colon\mathbf{CBCat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Proof

For a functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
,
\mathbf{T}(F)\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{D})
is defined on objects as
\mathbf{T}(F)(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}F(U_{i})
.
On arrows, it is defined inductively as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcl rcl}\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{1});\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{2})&\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{1})\oplus\mathbf{T}(F)(\mathfrak{t}_{2})\\
\mathbf{T}(F)(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{\mathbf{T}(F)(P)}&\mathbf{T}(F)(\rhd_{P})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rhd_{\mathbf{T}(F)(P)}\\
\mathbf{T}(F)(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{\mathbf{T}(F)(P)}&\mathbf{T}(F)(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\mathbf{T}(F)(P)}\\
\mathbf{T}(F)({id}_{U})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{id}_{\mathbf{T}(F)(U)}&\mathbf{T}(F)({id}_{0})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{id}_{\mathbf{T}(F)(0)}\\
\mathbf{T}(F)(\sigma_{P,Q}^{\oplus})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\sigma_{\mathbf{T}(F)(P),\mathbf{T}(F)(Q)}^{\oplus}&\mathbf{T}(F)(\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\underline{\smash{\overline{\;F(c)\vphantom{t}\;}}}\end{array}

Since by definition
\mathbf{T}(F)
is a monoidal functor preserving co-pcas and monoids, by Proposition
2
, it is a morphism of convex bicategories.
Thus, we have a functor
\mathbf{T}(-)\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{CBCat}
. Below, we prove the adjunction.

For all categories
\mathbf{C}
, the unit of the adjunction
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is the identity on objects functor mapping each arrow
c\colon U\to V
in
\mathbf{C}
into
\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}
. The axioms in (
Tape
) force
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}
to be a functor. Naturality of the unit is straightforward.

Now, take a convex biproduct category
\mathbf{D}
and consider a functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to U(\mathbf{D})
. By Proposition
1
,
\mathbf{D}
is a symmetric monoidal category where every object
X
carries a co-pca
(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X})
and a natural coherent monoid
(\rhd_{X},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X})
. One can use these structures to define
F^{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
inductively in the same way as
\mathbf{T}(F)
: e.g.,
F^{\sharp}(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F^{\sharp}(P)}
. The base case
F^{\sharp}(\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}})=F(c)
ensures that
\eta_{\mathbf{C}};F^{\sharp}=F
.
Since by definition
F^{\sharp}
is a strict symmetric monoidal functor preserving co-pcas and monoids, by Proposition
2
, it is a morphism of convex bicategories.

To prove uniqueness, take a morphism of convex bicategories
H\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
such that
\eta_{\mathbf{C}};H=F
, i.e.,
H(\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}})=F(c)
. By Proposition
2
,
H
has to preserve co-pcas and monoids. Thus
H=F^{\sharp}
.

Corollary 1

For all categories
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}^{+})
in
\mathbf{CBCat}
.

Proof

By composition of adjoints, their uniqueness and Theorems
2.1
,
4.1
and
5.2
.

The isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}^{+})
maps
\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}
into the

1\times 1
matrix
\begin{pmatrix}1\cdot c\end{pmatrix}
and
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}
,
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}
,
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}
,
\rhd_{U}
into the matrices defined in (
10
). Compositions and sums of arrows in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
are mapped into multiplications and direct sums of matrices as defined in (
8
) and (
9
). Same for identities and symmetries. For instance,
(^{\frac{1}{2}\;\,}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}\oplus{id}_{U});(\underline{\smash{\overline{\;a\vphantom{t}\;}}}\oplus\underline{\smash{\overline{\;ab\vphantom{t}\;}}}\oplus\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}});({id}_{U}\oplus\sigma^{\oplus}_{U,U});(\rhd_{U}\oplus{id}_{U})
is mapped into the matrix
M
of Example
2
.

The characterisation of
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}^{+})
by means of generators and equations provided by the above corollary, paves the way to study further equational theories (such as the one in Section
7
). The following result guarantees that the category obtained by quotienting
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
by additional axioms is still a convex biproduct category.

Proposition 6

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a category and
\sim
be a congruence relation (w.r.t
;
and
\oplus
) on
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
. Let
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
be the category obtained as the quotient of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
by
\sim
and
Q_{\sim}\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
be the functor mapping each arrow to its
\sim
-equivalence class.
Then
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
is a convex biproduct category and
Q_{\sim}
is a morphism of convex biproduct categories.

6
Probabilistic Tape Diagrams

Recall that a
monoidal signature
is a tuple
(\mathcal{S},\Sigma,\mathit{ar},\mathit{coar})
where
\mathcal{S}
is a set of basic sorts, hereafter denoted by
A,B,\dots
,
\Sigma
is a set of generators, denoted by
s,t\dots
, and
\mathit{ar},\mathit{coar}\colon\Sigma\to\mathcal{S}^{*}
assign to each symbol its arity and coarity (words over
\mathcal{S}
). From a monoidal signature
\Sigma
, one can freely generate the strict symmetric monoidal
\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
: objects are words in
\mathcal{S}^{*}
; arrows are
string diagrams

[
30
,
42
]
. These can be regarded as the terms generated by the following grammar (where
A,B\in\mathcal{S}
snd
s\in\Sigma
)

\begin{array}[]{rcccccccccccccccc}c&::=&{id}_{A}&\mid&{id}_{1}&\mid&s&\mid&\sigma^{\otimes}_{A,B}&\mid&c;c&\mid&c\otimes c\end{array}

(15)

modulo the axioms of strict symmetric monoidal categories. A
monoidal theory

\mathbb{T}=(\Sigma,E)
consists of a monoidal signature equipped with a set
E
of pairs of arrows of
\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
with same source and target. Let
=_{\mathbb{T}}
be equivalence relation on arrows of
\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
obtained as the congruence closure (w.r.t.
;
and
\otimes
) of
E
. We write
\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}
for the category obtained as the quotient of
\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
by
=_{\mathbb{T}}
.

The category
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}})
, obtained by applying the construction from Section
5
to
\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}
, coincides by definition with the category of
tape diagrams
from
[
2
]
. Objects of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}})
, are element of
(\mathcal{S}^{*})^{*}
which we often write as polynomials
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}{\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m_{i}}{A_{i,j}}}
.
We will call
monomials
of
P
the
n
words
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m_{i}}{A_{i,j}}
. For instance the monomials of
(A\otimes B)\oplus 1
are
A\otimes B
and
1
. We denote monomials by
U,V,\dots

Arrows of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}})
enjoy an intuitive diagrammatic representation specified by the following two layered grammar.

c

\Coloneqq

A
A

\,\mid\,

\,\mid\,

B
A
s

\,\mid\,

B
A
B
A

\,\mid\,

V
U
c
c

\,\mid\,

V
U

V^{\prime}
U^{\prime}
c

\,\mid\,

\mathfrak{t}

\Coloneqq

+_{p}

\,\mid\,

U

\,\mid\,

\,\mid\,

The first layer corresponds to (
15
) while the second to (
12
); diagrams from the first layer are string diagrams, those from the second are called tapes. Note that (a) string diagram can occur inside tapes, (b) string diagrams have type
U\to V
, for
U,V\in\mathcal{S}^{*}
and vertical composition corresponds to
\otimes
, (c) tapes have type
P\to Q
for
P,Q\in(\mathcal{S}^{*})^{*}
and vertical composition corresponds to
\oplus
.

The identity
{id}_{0}
is rendered as the empty tape
, while
{id}_{1}
is
: a tape filled with the empty string diagram. For a monomial
U\!=\!A_{1}\dots A_{n}
,
{id}_{U}
is depicted as a tape containing
n
wires labelled by
A_{i}
. For instance,
{id}_{AB}
is rendered as
B
B
A
A
. When clear from the context, we will simply represent it as a single wire
U
U
with the appropriate label.
Similarly, for a polynomial
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}{U_{i}}
,
{id}_{P}
is obtained as a vertical composition of tapes, as illustrated below on the left of (
16
).

The codiagonal
\rhd_{U}\colon U\oplus U\!\to\!U
is represented as a merging of tapes,
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}\colon U\oplus U\to U
as a splitting of tapes labeled with
p\in(0,1)
, the cobang
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}\colon 0\!\to\!U
is a tape closed on its left boundary, while
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\colon U\to 0
is closed on the right.
Exploiting the definitions in (
11
), we can construct
\rhd_{P},^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}
for arbitrary polynomials.
For example,
\rhd_{A\oplus B\oplus C}
and
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{AB\oplus B\oplus C}
are depicted as the second and third diagrams above. The last diagram is the tape for

{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{A};(^{q}\!\!\lhd_{A}\oplus{id}_{A});(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{A}\oplus\rhd_{A})
. For an arbitrary
\mathfrak{t}\colon P\to Q
we write

Figure 1
:
Axioms for tape diagrams.

It is now crucial to recall from
[
2
]
that
\otimes
can be defined not only on string diagrams but also on tapes: for
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon P\to Q
,
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon R\to S
,
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{t}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{L}_{P}({\mathfrak{t}_{2}});\mathsf{R}_{S}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}})
where
\mathsf{L}_{P}({\cdot})
,
\mathsf{R}_{S}({\cdot})
are the left and right whiskerings, defined as in Table 4 of
[
2
]
(reported in Table
6
in Appendix
0.A
). For objects

P=\bigoplus_{i}{U_{i}}
and
Q=\bigoplus_{j}{V_{j}}
,

P\otimes Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bigoplus_{i}{\bigoplus_{j}{U_{i}V_{j}}}
.

Theorem 27 in
[
2
]
guarantees that
(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}),\otimes,1)
is a symmetric monoidal category. Such category is monoidally enriched over
\mathbf{PCA}
:

\mathfrak{t}\otimes(\mathfrak{s}_{1}+_{p}\mathfrak{s}_{2})=(\mathfrak{t}\otimes\mathfrak{s}_{1})+_{p}(\mathfrak{t}\otimes\mathfrak{s}_{2})\qquad(\mathfrak{s}_{1}+_{p}\mathfrak{s}_{2})\otimes\mathfrak{t}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{t})+_{p}(\mathfrak{s}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{t})\qquad\star\otimes\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}\otimes\star

Most importantly, the two monoidal categories
(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}),\oplus,0)
and
(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}),\otimes,1)
interact through the laws of (right strict) rig category (see
[
32
,
29
]
).

Theorem 6.1
(From
[
2
]
)

(\,\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}),\oplus,\otimes,0,1\,)
is a right strict rig category.

By means of the isomorphism in Corollary
1
, we can equip
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+})
with an additional product
\otimes
. This is described as follows. On objects
P\otimes Q
is defined as above for tapes. On arrows, first define, for all
d_{1}\in\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+}[U_{1},V_{1}]
and
d_{2}\in\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+}[U_{2},V_{2}]
,
d_{1}\otimes^{+}d_{2}\in\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+}[U_{1}U_{2},V_{1}V_{2}]
as

d_{1}\otimes^{+}d_{2}(c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\{(c_{1},c_{2})\mid c_{1}\otimes c_{2}=c\}}d_{1}(c_{1})\cdot d_{2}(c_{2})\qquad\text{ for }c\in\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}[U_{1}U_{2},V_{1}V_{2}]\text{.}

(recall from Section
2
that
\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+}[U,V]=\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}[U,V])
). Then for matrices, it is defined as the Kronecker product using
\otimes^{+}
as multiplication.
More precisely, let
M\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}U_{i^{\prime}}
and
N\colon\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}V_{j}\to\bigoplus_{j^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}V_{j^{\prime}}
,
M\otimes N
is a matrix of size
n^{\prime}m^{\prime}\times nm
whose rows are indexed by pairs
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}\times\{1,\dots m^{\prime}\}
and columns by pairs
(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\{1,\dots m\}
. Assuming that
M_{i^{\prime},i}=p_{i^{\prime},i}\cdot d_{i^{\prime},i}

and
N_{j^{\prime},j}=q_{j^{\prime},j}\cdot e_{j^{\prime},j}
,
M\otimes N
is defined for all indexes
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),(i,j)
as

(M\otimes N)_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(p_{i^{\prime},i}\cdot q_{j^{\prime},j})\cdot(d_{i^{\prime},i}\otimes^{+}e_{j^{\prime},j})
.

Corollary 2

\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}})
and
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}}^{+})
as isomorphic as rig categories.

Arities and coarities are determined by the number of ports on the left and on the right: for instance
has arity
A^{2}=AA
and coarity
A
, while
has arity
A
and coarity
A^{0}=1
.
The first three generators represent operations and constants of boolean algebras (
\wedge
,
\neg
,
1
),
receives a boolean signal on the left and emits two copies on the right,
receives one signal on the left and discards it, while
p
denotes the distributions on the set
2\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{0,1\}
mapping
1\mapsto p
and
0\mapsto 1-p
.

Formally, the semantics of diagrams is the monoidal functor
\langle\!\langle{-}\rangle\!\rangle\colon\mathbf{Diag}_{PB}\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
defined on objects as
A^{n}\mapsto 2^{n}
; for arrows it is defined by first interpreting the generators as arrows in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})

Table 5
:
The monoidal theory of boolean algebras.

In
[
2
, Example 30]
, an encoding of probabilistic Boolean circuits into tape diagrams is presented.
Let
B
be the signature obtained from
PB
by dropping
p
and let
\mathbb{B}
be the monoidal theory in Table
5
consisting of the usual axioms of boolean algebras. For later use, it is worth to recall (see e.g.
[
40
, Theorem 3.2]
) that
\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Set}_{2}
, the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Set}
having as objects the sets
2^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We call boolean circuits the arrows of
\mathbf{Diag}_{B}
and probabilistic tapes of boolean circuits those of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B})
. The semantics of such tapes is given in
[
2
, Example 30]
by the morphism of rig categories
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B})\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
reported in Figure
2
. The encoding of probabilistic boolean circuits into tapes is the unique monoidal functor
\mathcal{E}(-)\colon(\mathbf{Diag}_{PB},\otimes,1)\to(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B}),\otimes,1)
mapping

As expected, the encoding preserves the semantics, in the sense that
\left\llbracket\mathcal{E}(c)\right\rrbracket=\langle\!\langle{c}\rangle\!\rangle
for all
c
in
\mathbf{Diag}_{PB}
.

​​​

\left\llbracket{id}_{A}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{2}

\left\llbracket{id}_{1}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{1}

\left\llbracket\sigma^{\otimes}_{A,A}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sigma^{\otimes}_{2,2}

\left\llbracket c;d\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\llbracket c\right\rrbracket;\left\llbracket d\right\rrbracket

\left\llbracket c\otimes d\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\llbracket c\right\rrbracket\otimes\left\llbracket d\right\rrbracket

\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\langle\!\langle{s}\rangle\!\rangle

\left\llbracket\,\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}\,\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\llbracket c\right\rrbracket

\left\llbracket{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{A^{n}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\;^{p}\!\!\lhd_{2^{n}}

\left\llbracket\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{A^{n}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{2^{n}}

\left\llbracket\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{A^{n}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{2^{n}}

\left\llbracket\rhd_{A^{n}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rhd_{2^{n}}

\left\llbracket{id}_{A^{n}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{2^{n}}

\left\llbracket{id}_{0}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}

\left\llbracket\sigma^{\oplus}_{A^{n},A^{m}}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sigma^{\oplus}_{2^{n},2^{m}}

\left\llbracket\mathfrak{s};\mathfrak{t}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\llbracket\mathfrak{s}\right\rrbracket;\left\llbracket\mathfrak{t}\right\rrbracket

\left\llbracket\mathfrak{s}\oplus\mathfrak{t}\right\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\llbracket\mathfrak{s}\right\rrbracket\oplus\left\llbracket\mathfrak{t}\right\rrbracket

Figure 2
:
The semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B})\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
. Here
s
is a generator in
B
.

) are enough to characterise the equivalence induced by
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
.

. Simple computations confirm that the axiom is sound:
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
maps the left and the right hand side of the equation in the same arrow in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
.

We write
\sim
for the congruence (w.r.t.
\oplus
,
\otimes
and
;
) on
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})
generated by the above axiom and
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}
for the category obtained as the quotient of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})
by
\sim
. We denote by
Q_{\sim}\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}
the functor mapping each tape into its
\sim
equivalence class and
Q_{\mathbb{B}}\colon\mathbf{Diag}_{B}\to\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}}
the functor mapping each diagram into its
=_{\mathbb{B}}
equivalence class.

Since the axioms are sound
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B})\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
can be factored as

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{T}(Q_{\mathbb{B}})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q_{\sim}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})}

for some functor
I
. Proving completeness amounts to prove that
I
is faithful.

By Corollary
1
we know that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}}^{+})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Set}_{2}^{+})
.
Now, let
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
having as objects the sets
2^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
J\colon\mathbf{Set}_{2}\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2}
be the obvious embedding. Since
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2}
is
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched, by Theorem
2.1
, there exists a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched functor
J^{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{Set}_{2}^{+}\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2}
. This enjoys the following key property.

Lemma 5

For all
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbf{Set}_{2}^{+}[1,2^{m}]
,
J^{\sharp}(d_{1})=J^{\sharp}(d_{2})
iff
d_{1}=d_{2}
.

Proof

It is convenient to give the explicit definition of
J^{\sharp}
. Recall that an arrow
d\colon 2^{n}\to 2^{m}
in
\mathbf{Set}_{2}^{+}
is a subprobability distribution on
\mathbf{Set}_{2}[2^{n},2^{m}]
, i.e., an element of
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{Set}_{2}[2^{n},2^{m}])
.
J^{\sharp}
is the identity on objects functor mapping
d\colon 2^{n}\to 2^{m}
, into the Kleisli arrow defined for all
b\in 2^{n},b^{\prime}\in 2^{m}
as
J^{\sharp}(d)(b^{\prime}|b)=\sum_{\{f\mid f(b)=b^{\prime}\}}d(f)
. It is trivial to see that for
n=0
,
J^{\sharp}
provides the obvious bijection

\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{Set}_{2}[1,2^{m}])\cong\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(2^{m})\cong\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2}[1,2^{m}]\text{.}

We call
F\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})
, the morphism of convex bicategories obtained by composing the isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Set}_{2}^{+})
with
\mathbf{StMat}(J^{\sharp})
.

We call
G\colon\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
the morphism of convex bicategories obtained by composing the embedding
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq}))
with the counit of the adjunction in Theorem
4.1
,
\epsilon_{\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})}\colon\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq}))\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
. The latter is, by Proposition
5
, full and faithful and, therefore, so is
G
.

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q_{\sim}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})}

Since
Q_{\sim}
is identity on objects and
G
is full and faithful, then by the property of orthogonal factorisation systems (see e.g.
[
38
]
), there exists a unique
H\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})
making the above diagram commute. Below we prove that
H
is faithful to conclude that also
I
is faithful.

Hereafter, we write
\mathbf{B}[1,A^{n}]
for the set

-ary boolean vectors, i.e., all those tapes in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})[1,A^{n}]
of the form
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}
for

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.89148pt}{-5.33488pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.89148pt}{-5.33488pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.89148pt}{5.33488pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
. The following is proved by a simple inductive argument.

Lemma 6

Let
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\in\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})[A^{n},A^{m}]
. If, for all
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbf{B}[1,A^{n}]
,
\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{s}\sim\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{t}
, then
\mathfrak{s}\sim\mathfrak{t}
.

Theorem 7.1

H\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})_{2})
is faithful.

Proof

Let
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon A^{n}\to A^{m}
be arrows in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})
. Denote by
[\mathfrak{s}]_{\sim}
and
[\mathfrak{t}]_{\sim}
their
\sim
equivalence classes.

\displaystyle H([\mathfrak{s}]_{\sim})=H([\mathfrak{t}]_{\sim})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow F(\mathfrak{s})=F(\mathfrak{t})

(
F=Q_{\sim};H
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbf{B}[1,A^{n}]\text{, }F(\mathfrak{b});F(\mathfrak{s})=F(\mathfrak{b});F(\mathfrak{t})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbf{B}[1,A^{n}]\text{, }F(\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{s})=F(\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{t})

(Functoriality)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbf{B}[1,A^{n}]\text{, }\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{t}

(Lemma
5
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathfrak{s}\sim\mathfrak{t}

(Lemma
6
)

The above derivation proves that
H
is faithful for arrows of type
A^{n}\to A^{m}
. This fact easily entails that
H
is faithful for arrows
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon A^{k}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A^{m_{i}}
.
Indeed, by Lemma
3
and Theorem
5.1
,

\mathfrak{s}=\langle\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\rangle_{\vec{q}}\quad\mathfrak{t}=\langle\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n}\rangle_{\vec{p}}

(18)

for some
\vec{q}=q_{1},\dots,q_{n}
,
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\colon A^{k}\to A^{m_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon A^{k}\to A^{m_{i}}
. Thus

\displaystyle H([\mathfrak{s}]_{\sim})=H([\mathfrak{t}]_{\sim})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow F(\mathfrak{s})=F(\mathfrak{t})

(
F=Q_{\sim};H
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }i\text{, }F(\mathfrak{s});F(\pi_{i})=F(\mathfrak{t});F(\pi_{i})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }i\text{, }F(\mathfrak{s};\pi_{i})=F(\mathfrak{t};\pi_{i})

(Functoriality)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }i\text{, }\mathfrak{s};\pi_{i}\sim\mathfrak{t};\pi_{i}

(Previous implication)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }i\text{, }q_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{i}\sim p_{i}\cdot t_{i}

(
18
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{for all }i\text{, }[q_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{i}]_{\sim}=[p_{i}\cdot t_{i}]_{i}

By Proposition
6
,
[\mathfrak{s}]_{\sim}=\langle[\mathfrak{s}_{1}]_{\sim},\dots,[\mathfrak{s}_{n}]_{\sim}\rangle_{\vec{q}}
and
[\mathfrak{t}]_{\sim}=\langle[\mathfrak{t}_{1}]_{\sim},\dots,[\mathfrak{t}_{n}]_{\sim}\rangle_{\vec{p}}
. Since for all
i
,
[\mathfrak{t}];[\pi_{i}]_{\sim}
and
[\mathfrak{s}];[\pi_{i}]_{\sim}
are both equal to
p_{i}\cdot[\mathfrak{t}_{i}]_{\sim}=[p_{i}\cdot t_{i}]_{\sim}=[q_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{i}]_{\sim}
, the universal property of n-ary convex products in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}
implies that
[\mathfrak{s}]_{\sim}=[\mathfrak{t}]_{\sim}
.

For arrows of arbitrary type
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{o}A^{k_{j}}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A^{m_{i}}
, one can easily rely on the universal property of coproducts and the case that we just proved.

Corollary 3

I\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})_{\sim}\to\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
is faithful.

Remark 2

Our proof of completeness for probabilistic Boolean circuits via tape diagrams differs substantially from the approach in
[
40
]
, which is based on a normal-form argument. Instead, we rely on the isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}}^{+})
established in Corollary
1
.
The axioms for tape diagrams involve only monoid and co-pca structures, the laws of Boolean algebra, and the additional multiplexer axiom (
17
). By contrast, the axioms for
p
in
[
40
]
are more elaborate, as they act directly on the probabilistic structure. In particular, axioms E1, E3, and E4 of
[
40
]
follow readily from the monoid, co-pca, and Boolean axioms, while axiom E2 (see Figure
3
) requires the multiplexer axiom (
17
) from
[
13
]
.

Figure 3
:
Axiom E2 from
[
40
]
.

8
Conclusion

We have introduced the notion of convex biproduct categories (Def.
2
) and the construction
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
of stochastic matrices over a
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched category
\mathbf{C}
, showing that
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is the free convex biproduct category generated by
\mathbf{C}
(Thm.
4.1
).

We then presented a syntactic construction: for any category
\mathbf{C}
, the category
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is obtained by freely adding a monoidal structure with natural monoids and co-pointed convex algebras. We showed that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is the free convex biproduct category over
\mathbf{C}
(Thm.
5.2
). Combining this with a known result (Thm.
2.1
), we derived the isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}^{+})
(Cor.
1
), where arrows in
\mathbf{C}^{+}
are subdistributions of arrows in
\mathbf{C}
.

This construction is particularly relevant when
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}
, a category of string diagrams: in this case,
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})
yields the category of probabilistic tape diagrams introduced in
[
2
]
. The isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}^{+})
thus provides a concrete interpretation of tape diagrams as stochastic matrices whose entries are probability subdistributions over string diagrams.

Relying on
[
2
, Ex. 30]
, we applied this framework to the complete axiomatisation of probabilistic Boolean circuits in
[
40
]
. We proposed an alternative axiomatisation, combining the axioms for Boolean circuits, those for probabilistic tapes, and one additional law from
[
13
]
. Thanks to the isomorphism
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma})\cong\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{Diag}_{\Sigma}^{+})
, the resulting completeness proof becomes conceptually simpler and structurally more transparent.

Related and future work.
The construction of stochastic matrices over pca-enriched categories is conceptually akin to the classical construction of matrices over categories enriched in commutative monoids
[
34
,
12
]
. However, while the latter is straightforward enough to be left as an exercise in
[
34
]
, the former involves significantly more work. In particular, finite biproduct categories enjoy a bijective correspondence between arrows
f\colon A\oplus B\to C\oplus D
and quadruples of morphisms
(f_{A,C},f_{B,C},f_{A,D},f_{B,D})
, where each
f_{X,Y}\colon X\to Y
. This correspondence breaks down in convex biproduct categories. Our probabilistic tape diagrams appear to be closely related to effectuses
[
11
,
9
]
.
Effectuses are based on finitely
partially additive categories

[
1
]
, that is, categories enriched over partial commutative monoids, rather than pointed convex algebras. A precise understanding of the relationship between these two frameworks constitutes an interesting direction for future research.

In
[
46
]
, a general framework is introduced to enrich string diagrams over arbitrary algebraic theories. This approach fundamentally differs from the tape-based construction in
[
2
]
, in that it does not account for the structure induced by the coproduct
\oplus
. As a consequence, the correspondence with matrix-based semantics does not arise in
[
46
]
.

We plan to extend probabilistic tape diagrams with traces for
\oplus
, following the approach developed in
[
3
]
. That work showed that a specific property of monoidal traces–called
uniformity
–corresponds to reasoning by invariants in Hoare-style program logics
[
25
]
. Interestingly, as demonstrated in
[
26
]
, uniformity holds for traces over
\oplus
in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
. Preliminary results suggest that, in the probabilistic setting, invariants correspond to sub-martingales, thus offering a promising link between diagrammatic semantics and probabilistic program verification.

Moreover, probabilistic tape diagrams extended with traces are expressive enough to encode probabilistic regular expressions from
[
41
]
, which provide a complete axiomatisation for the regular behaviours of generative probabilistic systems–that is, coalgebras in
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})

[
24
]
. We expect that tapes may again offer a more principled and modular axiomatisation, akin to Kleene algebra in
[
31
]
. Interestingly, the completeness proof in
[
31
]
relies heavily on matrix representations over languages; as we hint in Example
2
, our tape formalism naturally supports stochastic matrices over probabilistic languages.

To conclude, we mention that extending our axiomatisation to probabilistic circuits with
(used in
[
40
]
for explicit conditioning) is a challenging future work. Indeed, our proof crucially relies on the well-known isomorphism
\mathbf{Diag}_{\mathbb{B}}\cong\mathbf{Set}_{2}
, while we are not aware of a similar result for partial functions, necessary to accommodate
.

\hline\cr\delta^{l}_{0,Q,R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}\vphantom{\sigma^{\otimes}_{P,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}}\hskip 14.72241pt\delta^{l}_{U\oplus P^{\prime},Q,R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}({id}_{U\otimes(Q\oplus R)}\oplus\delta^{l}_{P^{\prime},Q,R});({id}_{U\otimes Q}\oplus\sigma^{\oplus}_{U\otimes R,P^{\prime}\otimes Q}\oplus{id}_{P^{\prime}\otimes R})\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\sigma^{\otimes}_{P,Q}\colon P\otimes Q\to Q\otimes P,\text{ with }P=\bigoplus_{i}{U_{i}}\\
\hline\cr\sigma^{\otimes}_{P,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{0}\hskip 14.72241pt\sigma^{\otimes}_{P,V\oplus Q^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\delta^{l}_{P,V,Q^{\prime}};(\bigoplus_{i}{\overline{\underline{\;\sigma_{U_{i},V}\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}}\oplus\sigma^{\otimes}_{P,Q^{\prime}})\end{array}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{rclrcl|rclrcl}\hline\cr\mathsf{L}_{U}({{id}_{0}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{id}_{0}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}})\oplus\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\mathsf{R}_{U}({{id}_{0}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{id}_{0}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}})\oplus\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{2}})\\
\mathsf{L}_{U}({\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\overline{\underline{\;{id}_{U}\otimes c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}});\mathsf{L}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\overline{\underline{\;c\otimes{id}_{U}\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{1}});\mathsf{R}_{U}({\mathfrak{t}_{2}})\\

\mathsf{L}_{U}({{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{UV}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{UV}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{VU}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{VU}\\
\mathsf{L}_{U}({\rhd_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rhd_{UV}&\mathsf{L}_{U}({\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{UV}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\rhd_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rhd_{VU}&\mathsf{R}_{U}({\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{V}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{VU}\\
