Appendix 0.B
Some examples of convex biproduct categories

In this appendix we illustrare several examples of convex biproduct categories: the Kleisli category for the subdistribution monad, its continuous version and, finally, a result about finitely partially additive categories.

0.B.1
The Kleisli category of the subdistribution monad

Proposition 7

\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
is a convex biproduct category

Proof

Coproducts are induced by the coproducts in
\mathbf{Set}
, and the empty set
0
is both initial and final. Indeed, for every object
X
, the unique arrow
X\to 0
sends an element
x\in X
into the null subdistribution (which is the unique element of
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(0)
).

\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
is
\mathbf{PCA}
-enriched since, for every
X,Y\in\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})
, the hom-set
\mathcal{KL}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq})[X,Y]
is a pointed convex algebra with the operations defined as follows:
+_{p}(f,g)(y|x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}p\cdot f(y|x)+(1-p)\cdot g(y|x)
for all
f,g\colon X\to Y
and
p\in(0,1)
, and
\star_{X,Y}(y|x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}0
(i.e., it is the arrow which sends every
x
into the null subdistribution over
Y
). An easy calculation shows that arrow composition is a morphism of pointed convex algebras, i.e., it preserves the operations
+_{p}
and
\star_{X,Y}
. For instance
(f+_{p}g);h=(f;h+_{p}g;h)
for all
f,g\colon X\to Y
and
h\colon Y\to Z
is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle((f+_{p}g);h)(z|x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}(f+_{p}g)(y|x)\cdot h(z|y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}(p\cdot f(y|x)+(1-p)\cdot g(y|x))\cdot h(z|y)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sum_{y\in Y}f(y|x)\cdot h(z|y)+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{y\in Y}g(y|x)\cdot h(z|y)

\displaystyle=p\cdot(f;h)(z|x)+(1-p)\cdot(g;h)(z|x)

\displaystyle=(f;h+_{p}g;h)(z|x)\text{.}

Now observe that
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
with the projections
\pi_{i}\colon X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\to X_{i}
for
i=1,2
given by

\pi_{1}(x_{1}|z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{1}}&z=\iota_{1}(x_{1})\\
0&otherwise\end{cases}\qquad\pi_{2}(x_{2}|z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{2}}&z=\iota_{2}(x_{2})\\
0&otherwise\end{cases}

is a convex product of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. Indeed, given arrows
f_{1}\colon A\to X_{1}
and
f_{2}\colon A\to X_{2}
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in(0,1)
such that
p_{1}+p_{2}\leq 1
, there exists an arrow
\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}}\colon A\to X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
defined as follows:

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}}(z|a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}p_{1}\cdot f_{1}(x_{1}|a)&z=\iota_{1}(x_{1})\\
p_{2}\cdot f_{2}(x_{2}|a)&z=\iota_{2}(x_{2})\text{.}\end{cases}

Observe that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}};\pi_{i}(x_{i}|a)=

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in X_{1}+X_{2}}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}}(z|a)\cdot\pi_{i}(x_{i}|z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}}(\iota_{i}(x_{i})|a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{i}\cdot f_{i}(x_{i}|a)

namely, it holds that
(\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}});\pi_{i}=p_{i}\cdot f_{i}
. This is the unique arrow with such property since, for any other arrow
h^{\prime}\colon A\to X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
such that
h^{\prime};\pi_{i}=p_{i}\cdot f_{i}
for all
i=1,2
it follows that

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(\iota_{1}(x_{1})|a)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}(\iota_{1}(x_{1})|a)\cdot 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}h^{\prime}(z|a)\cdot\pi_{1}(x_{1}|z)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime};\pi_{1}(x_{1}|a)

\displaystyle=p_{1}\cdot f_{1}(\iota_{1}(x_{1})|a)

and similarly for
\pi_{2}
one obtains that
h^{\prime}(\iota_{2}(x_{2})|a)=p_{2}\cdot f_{2}(\iota_{2}(x_{2})|a)
, i.e.,
h^{\prime}=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{p_{1},p_{2}}
.
Simple computations confirms that (
5
) and (
6
) hold.

0.B.2
Substochastic Markov kernels on standard Borel spaces

Consider the category of
standard Borel spaces
and measurable functions denoted with
\mathbf{BorelMeas}
.
Given a standard Borel space
(X,\Sigma_{X})
, denote with
\mathcal{G}_{\leq}(X)
the set of subprobability measures on
X
, i.e. measurable functions
\mu:(X,\Sigma_{X})\to([0,1],\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
such that
\mu(X)\leq 1
, where
\mathcal{B}([0,1])
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
[0,1]
. This assignment extends to a functor
\mathcal{G}_{\leq}:\mathbf{BorelMeas}\to\mathbf{BorelMeas}
which corresponds to the subdistribution version of the
Giry monad

[
23
]
. The functor
\mathcal{G}_{\leq}
is a symmetric monoidal monad and its Kleisli category,
\mathcal{KL}({\mathcal{G}_{\leq}})
, is the category of standard Borel spaces and substochastic Markov kernels. A substochastic Markov kernel from
(X,\Sigma_{X})
to
(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
is a function
f\colon\Sigma_{Y}\times X\to[0,1]
such that for all
x\in X
,
f(-|x)\colon\Sigma_{Y}\to[0,1]
is a subprobability measure on
Y
and for all
U\in\Sigma_{Y}
,
f(U|-)\colon X\to[0,1]
is a measurable function.
The identity arrow on
(X,\Sigma_{X})
is the Markov kernel
\delta_{X}
such that for all
x\in X
and
U\in\Sigma_{X}
,
\delta_{X}(U|x)=1
if
x\in U
and
0
otherwise. The composition of arrows in
\mathcal{KL}({\mathcal{G}_{\leq}})
is given by the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation: for all substochastic Markov kernels
f\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
and
g\colon(Y,\Sigma_{Y})\to(Z,\Sigma_{Z})
,
f;g\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Z,\Sigma_{Z})
is defined as the Markov kernel such that for all
x\in X
and
U\in\Sigma_{Z}
,

(f;g)(U|x)\coloneqq\int_{Y}g(U|y)\,f(dy|x)

Proposition 8

\mathcal{KL}({\mathcal{G}_{\leq}})
is a convex biproduct category.

Proof

Coproducts in
\mathcal{KL}({\mathcal{G}_{\leq}})
are given by the coproducts in
\mathbf{BorelMeas}
, i.e. the disjoint union of standard Borel spaces. The initial and terminal object is the empty standard Borel space. The
\mathbf{PCA}
-enrichment is given by the pointwise convex combination of Markov kernels: for all Markov kernels
f,g\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
and
p\in[0,1]
,
f+_{p}g\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
is defined for all
x\in X
and
U\in\Sigma_{Y}
as
f+_{p}g(U|x)\coloneqq p\cdot f(U|x)+(1-p)\cdot g(U|x)
. The zero arrow
\star_{(X,\Sigma_{X}),(Y,\Sigma_{Y})}\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
is the Markov kernel such that for all
x\in X
and
U\in\Sigma_{Y}
,
\star_{(X,\Sigma_{X}),(Y,\Sigma_{Y})}(U|x)=0
. The axioms of
\mathbf{PCA}
are satisfied since they are so in
[0,1]
. A simple computation shows that
+_{p}
and
\star
are compatible with composition. For instance, as done in Proposition
7
, we obtain that
(f+_{p}g);h=f;h+_{p}g;h
for all Markov kernels
f,g\colon(X,\Sigma_{X})\to(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
,
h\colon(Y,\Sigma_{Y})\to(Z,\Sigma_{Z})
and
p\in[0,1]
in the following way

\displaystyle\big((f+_{p}g);h\big)(C\mid x)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}h(C\mid y)\,(f+_{p}g)(dy\mid x)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}h(C\mid y)\,\big(p\,f(dy\mid x)+(1-p)\,g(dy\mid x)\big)

\displaystyle=p\int_{Y}h(C\mid y)\,f(dy\mid x)\;+\;(1-p)\int_{Y}h(C\mid y)\,g(dy\mid x)

(linearity of the integral)

\displaystyle=p\,(f;h)(C\mid x)\;+\;(1-p)\,(g;h)(C\mid x)

\displaystyle=\big((f;h)+_{p}(g;h)\big)(C\mid x).

Now we prove that
(X_{1},\Sigma_{X_{1}})\overset{\pi_{1}}{\leftarrow}(X_{1},\Sigma_{X_{1}})\oplus(X_{2},\Sigma_{X_{2}})\overset{\pi_{2}}{\rightarrow}(X_{2},\Sigma_{X_{2}})
is a binary convex product in
\mathcal{KL}({\mathcal{G}_{\leq}})
. The Markov kernels
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are defined as follows: for all
z\in X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
and
U\in\Sigma_{X_{1}}
,
\pi_{1}(U|z)=1
if
z\in\iota_{1}(U)
and
0
otherwise; for all
z\in X\oplus Y
and
V\in\Sigma_{X_{2}}
,
\pi_{2}(V|z)=1
if
z\in\iota_{2}(V)
and
0
otherwise. Given
f\colon(A,\Sigma_{A})\to(X_{1},\Sigma_{X_{1}})
,
g\colon(A,\Sigma_{A})\to(X_{2},\Sigma_{X_{2}})
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
p_{1}+p_{2}\leq 1
, we prove that there is a unique Markov kernel
h\colon(A,\Sigma_{A})\to(X_{1},\Sigma_{X_{1}})\oplus(X_{2},\Sigma_{X_{2}})
such that
h;\pi_{1}=p_{1}\cdot f
and
h;\pi_{2}=p_{2}\cdot g
. Existence is provided by the Markov kernel
h
defined as follows: for all
a\in A
,
U\in\Sigma_{X_{1}}
and
V\in\Sigma_{X_{2}}
,

h(\iota_{1}(U)|a)\coloneqq p_{1}\cdot f(U|a)\quad h(\iota_{2}(V)|a)\coloneqq p_{2}\cdot g(V|a)

where for a generic element of
\Sigma_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}

h
is extended by countable additivity. Uniqueness is proved as follows: suppose that there is also
h^{\prime}
with the same property. Then, for all
a\in A
,
U\in\Sigma_{X_{1}}
and
V\in\Sigma_{X_{2}}
, then

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(\iota_{1}(U)\mid a)

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\iota_{1}(U)}(z)\,h^{\prime}(dz\mid a)

(def. of integration w.r.t.
h^{\prime}(\cdot\mid a)
)

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}\pi_{1}(U\mid z)\,h^{\prime}(dz\mid a)

(def. of indicator function)

\displaystyle=(h^{\prime};\pi_{1})(U\mid a)

(kernel composition)

\displaystyle=(p_{1}\cdot k_{1})(U\mid a).

(hp.
h^{\prime};\pi_{1}=p_{1}k_{1}
)

similarly
h^{\prime}(\iota_{2}(V)|a)=(p_{2}\cdot g)(V|a)
. Since every element of
\Sigma_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}
is obtained by countable unions of elements of the form
\iota_{1}(U)
and
\iota_{2}(V)
,
h^{\prime}
is completely determined by the above equalities, hence
h^{\prime}=h
.

Appendix 0.C
Appendix to Section
2

Proof
(Proof of Theorem
2.1
)

The result follows from more general results about enriching on arbitrary algebraic theories: see for instance
[
8
, Prop. 6.4.7]
or
[
46
, Cor. 1]
. It is anyway convenient to show the involved structures.

We first illustrate the definition of
F^{+}\colon\mathbf{C}^{+}\to\mathbf{D}^{+}
for a functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
. For all objects
X
,
F^{+}
is defined as
F^{+}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}F(X)
; For all
d\in\mathbf{C}^{+}[X,Y]
,
F^{+}(d)\in\mathbf{D}^{+}[FX,FY]
is defined for all
h\colon FX\to FY
as
F^{+}(d)(g)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\{f\in\mathbf{C}[X,Y]|F(f)=g\}}d(f)
.
One can easily check that the functor is PCA-enriched.

For all categories
\mathbf{C}
, the unit
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{+}
is the functor acting as identity on objects and mapping any arrow
f\in\mathbf{C}[X,Y]
into
\delta_{f}\in\mathbf{C^{+}}[X,Y]
.

Now, given a PCA-enriched category
\mathbf{D}
and a functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to U(\mathbf{D})
, one can define a functor
F^{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{C}^{+}\to\mathbf{D}
as follows: for all objects
X
,
F^{\sharp}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}F(X)
, for all arrow
d\in\mathbf{C}^{+}[X,Y]
,
F^{\sharp}(d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\sum_{f\in\mathbf{C}[X,Y]}d(f)\cdot\delta_{F(f)}
.

Appendix 0.D
Appendix to Section

Proof
(Proof of Lemma
2
)

By induction on
n
, we show that
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a convex product of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. For
n=0
, observe that the object
0
is final by Definition
2
.
For
n+1
assume by induction hypothesis that
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a convex product of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
with projection
\pi_{i}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\to X_{i}
. Since
\mathbf{C}
has binary convex product we can take the convex product
(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}
with projections
\pi^{\prime}_{1}\colon(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}\colon(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}\to X_{n+1}
. We claim that
(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}
with projections
\pi^{\prime\prime}_{i}\colon(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}\to X_{i}
defined for all
i\in 1\dots n+1
as

\pi^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\pi_{1}^{\prime};\pi_{i}&i\in 1,\dots n\\
\pi_{2}^{\prime}&i=n+1\end{cases}

is a convex product of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n+1}
.
To check the universal property, let
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n+1}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}p_{i}\leq 1
and
f_{i}\colon A\to X_{i}
. Take
\vec{q}=q_{1},\dots,q_{n}
with
q_{i}=\frac{p_{i}}{1-p_{n+1}}
, since

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a
n
-ary convex product by induction hypothesis, there exists an arrow
\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\rangle_{\vec{q}}\colon A\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
such that
p_{i}\cdot f_{i}=\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\rangle_{\vec{q}};\pi_{i}
. For the
n+1
-ary, we construct the mediating morphism
h\colon A\to(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})\oplus X_{n+1}
as
\langle\,\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\rangle_{\vec{q}}\,,\,f_{n+1}\,\rangle_{1-p_{n+1},p_{n+1}}
.

Lemma 8

In a convex biproduct category,

Lemma 9

In a convex biproduct category, the coherence axioms in Figure
7
hold.

Proof

(Coh2), (Coh3) and (Coh4) follow from the fact that
0
is both initial and final. (Coh1) follows from the naturality and the universal property of coproducts.

Let
s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{X,X,Y\oplus Y};({{id}_{X}\oplus\alpha^{-}_{X,Y,Y}});({{id}_{X}\oplus(\sigma_{X,Y}^{\oplus}\oplus{id}_{Y})});({id}_{X}\oplus\alpha_{Y,X,Y});\alpha^{-}_{X,Y,X\oplus Y}
. Let

\iota_{1}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}\colon X\oplus X\to(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)\text{ and }\iota_{2}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}\colon Y\oplus Y\to(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)

be the injections. By using the definitions of the structural isomorphisms induced by the coproducts, one can easily check that

\iota_{1}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)};s=\iota_{1}\oplus\iota_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota_{2}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)};s=\iota_{2}\oplus\iota_{2}\text{.}

(19)

Then

\displaystyle\iota_{1};(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}\oplus\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y});s

\displaystyle=\iota_{1};[^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};\iota_{1}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}\,,\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y};\iota_{2}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}];s

(def. of
\oplus
)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};\iota_{1}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)};s

(coproduct)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(\iota_{1}\oplus\iota_{1})

(
19
)

\displaystyle=\iota_{1};\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X\oplus Y}

(Lemma
8
)

and

\displaystyle\iota_{2};(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}\oplus\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y});s

\displaystyle=\iota_{2};[^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};\iota_{1}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}\,,\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y};\iota_{2}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)}];s

(def. of
\oplus
)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y};\iota_{2}^{(X\oplus X)\oplus(Y\oplus Y)};s

(coproduct)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(\iota_{2}\oplus\iota_{2})

(
19
)

\displaystyle=\iota_{2};\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X\oplus Y}

(Lemma
8
)

Hence, the universal property of coproducts implies that
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X\oplus Y}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}\oplus^{p}\!\!\lhd_{Y};s
.

Lemma 10

In a convex biproduct category the following hold:

1.

p\cdot^{q}\!\!\lhd_{X}=\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{pq,p(1-q)}
.

2.

{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};({id}_{X}\oplus q\cdot{id}_{X})=\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,(1-p)q}

Proof

We prove below item by item.

Proposition 9

Let
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a functor between convex biproduct categories. If
F
preserves finite coproducts then

F
preserves convex binary products.

Proof

Preservation of finite coproducts is equivalent to preservation of binary coproducts and initial object. Hence, assume that
F(X)\overset{F(\iota_{1})}{\to}F(X\oplus Y)\overset{F(\iota_{2})}{\leftarrow}
is a coproduct of
F(X)
and
F(Y)
, which is equivalent to requiring that the arrow
[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})]\colon F(X)\oplus F(Y)\to F(X\oplus Y)
is an isomorphism (call
k
the inverse). And assume also that
F(0_{\mathbf{C}})
is initial in
\mathbf{D}
(hence
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(0_{\mathbf{C}})}
is an isomorphism). Now observe that

\displaystyle F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})

\displaystyle=F({id}_{X}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Y};\rho_{X})

(Lemma
7
.
2
)

\displaystyle=F({id}_{X}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Y});F([{id}_{X},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}])

(def.
\rho
)

\displaystyle=F([{id}_{X},\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Y};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}])

(Coproducts)

\displaystyle=k;[{id}_{F(X)},F(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Y};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X})]

(coproducts and
k
iso)

\displaystyle=k;({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(Y)});[{id}_{F(X)},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(X)}]

(
F(0_{\mathbf{C}})\cong 0_{\mathbf{D}}
)

\displaystyle=k;\pi_{1}^{F(X)\oplus F(Y)}

(def
\rho
)

and similarly
F(\pi_{2}^{X\oplus Y})=k;\pi_{2}^{F(X)\oplus F(Y)}
. We can now prove that
F(X\oplus Y)
with projections
F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})
and
F(\pi_{2}^{X\oplus Y})
is a convex binary product of
F(X)
and
F(Y)
. Let
f\colon A\to F(X)
and
g\colon A\to F(Y)
and
p\in(0,1)
and take the arrow
\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})]\colon A\to F(X\oplus Y)
, where
\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p}\colon A\to F(X)\oplus F(Y)
is the unique arrow induced bu convex binary products in
\mathbf{D}
. Now, since
F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})=k;\pi_{1}^{F(X\oplus F(Y))}
it follows that

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})];F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})

\displaystyle=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})];k;\pi_{1}^{F(X\oplus F(Y))}

\displaystyle=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};\pi_{1}^{F(X)\oplus F(Y)}

(
k
inverse)

\displaystyle=p\cdot f

(convex products)

and similarly
\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})];F(\pi_{2}^{X\oplus Y})=(1-p)\cdot g
. For the uniqueness, if
h:A\to F(X\oplus Y)
is such that
h;F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})=p\cdot f
and
h;F(\pi_{2}^{X\oplus Y})=(1-p)\cdot g
then
h;k=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p}
. Post-composition with
[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})]
gives
h=\langle f,g\rangle_{p,1-p};[F(\iota_{1}),F(\iota_{2})]
.

Proof
(Proof of Proposition
2
)

By Fox’s theorem we know that every finite coproduct category
\mathbf{C}
(hence any convex biproduct category) correspond to monoidal category
(\mathbf{C},\oplus_{\mathbf{C}},0_{\mathbf{C}})
in which every object
X
is equipped with a coherent and natural monoid structure
(X,\rhd_{X},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X})
. Moreover, a functor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
between finite coproducts categories preserves finite coproducts if and only if seen as a monoidal funtor
F\colon(\mathbf{C},\oplus_{\mathbf{C}},0_{\mathbf{C}})\to(\mathbf{D},\oplus_{\mathbf{D}},0_{\mathbf{D}})
it is strong monoidal and preserves monoids

\psi_{X,X};F(\rhd_{X})=\rhd_{F(X)}\qquad\psi_{0};F(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(X)}

where
\psi\colon\oplus_{\mathbf{C}}\circ(F\times F)\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}F\circ\oplus_{\mathbf{D}}
and
\psi_{0}\colon 0_{\mathbf{D}}\overset{\cong}{\to}F(0_{\mathbf{C}})
are given by the strong monoidal structure. For any convex biproduct category, Proposition
1
implies that every object
X
is equipped with a natural and coherent co-pca structure
(X,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X},\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X})
. In this case, a strong monoidal functor that preserves monoids between monoidal categories, in which every object has the structure of a coherent and natural monoid and co-pca, also preserves
\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\psi_{0}^{-1};\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{0}

(
Coh3
in Figure
7
)

\displaystyle=\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(0)}

(
Nat. co-pca1
)

Hence,
\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X}
is preserved:
F(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X});\psi_{0}^{-1}=F(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X});\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(0)}=\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)}
.

Moreover, observe that a convex biproduct category
\mathbf{C}
corresponds to a monoidal category

(\mathbf{C},\oplus_{\mathbf{C}},0_{\mathbf{C}})
in which every object is equipped with coherent and natural monoid and co-pca structures and moreover it holds that given
f:X\to A
and
g:X\to B
then for every
h\colon X\to A\oplus B

\begin{cases}h;({id}_{A}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{B})=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(f\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{B})\\
h;(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{A}\oplus{id}_{B})=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{A}\oplus g)\end{cases}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad h=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(f\oplus g)

(20)

since
\mathbf{C}
has convex binary products.
Now if
F
is PCA-enriched, then

\displaystyle F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});\psi_{X,X}^{-1};({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)})

\displaystyle=F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});\psi_{X,X}^{-1};({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)});\rho_{F(X)};\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

\displaystyle=F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});\psi_{X,X}^{-1};({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)});({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(X)});\rhd_{F(X)};\rho_{F(X)}^{-1}

(Def.
\rho
)

\displaystyle=F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});F({id}_{X}\oplus(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}));\psi_{X,X}^{-1};\rhd_{F(X)};\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

(Nat.
\psi
)

\displaystyle=F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});F({id}_{X}\oplus(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{X}));F(\rhd_{X});\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

(
\psi_{X,X};F(\rhd_{X})=\rhd_{F(X)}
)

\displaystyle=F(p\cdot{id}_{X});\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

(Eq.
6
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot{id}_{F(X)};\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

(PCA-enrichment)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)};({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)});\rho_{F(X)};\rho^{-1}_{F(X)}

(Eq.
6
+ Def.
\rho
+ Lemma
7
.1)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)};({id}_{F(X)}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)})

and similarly one can prove that
F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});\psi_{X,X}^{-1};(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)}\oplus{id}_{F(X)})=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)};(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)}\oplus{id}_{F(X)})
. Hence, by property (
20
) it follows that
F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X});\psi_{X,X}^{-1}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)}
.
Vice versa, if
F\colon(\mathbf{C},\oplus_{\mathbf{C}},0_{\mathbf{C}})\to(\mathbf{D},\oplus_{\mathbf{D}},0_{\mathbf{D}})
preserves also diagonals
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
then it is PCA-enriched since

\displaystyle F(f+_{p}g)

\displaystyle=F(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X};(f\oplus g);\rhd_{X})

(Eq.
6
)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)};\psi_{X,X};F(f\oplus g;\rhd_{X})

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(X)};F(f)\oplus F(g);\rhd_{F(X)}

\displaystyle=F(f)+_{p}F(g)

(Eq.
6
)

Similarly, the preservation of
\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X}
and
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Y}
implies that
F(\star_{X,Y})=\star_{F(X),F(Y)}
.

Corollary 4

Let
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a functor between convex biproduct categories. If
F
preserves finite coproducts then

F
preserves n-ary convex products.

Proof

It follows from Proposition
9
and Lemma
2
which shows how to produce n-ary convex products from binary ones.

Proposition 10

Let
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a functor between convex biproduct categories that preserves finite coproducts, then
F
is PCA-enriched if and only if
F
preserves
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{X}
for every
X\in\mathbf{C}
.

Proof

Using the notation of proposition
9
, if
F
is PCA-enriched, then

\displaystyle F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p});k;\pi_{1}^{F(X)\oplus F(Y)}

\displaystyle=F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p});F(\pi_{1}^{X\oplus Y})

(Prop.
9
)

\displaystyle=F(p\cdot{id}_{X})=p\cdot{id}_{F(X)}

(PCA-enrichment)

and similarly
F(\langle id,id\rangle_{p,1-p});k;\pi_{1}^{F(X)\oplus F(Y)}=(1-p)\cdot{id}_{F(X)}
. Hence, the universal property of convex products in
\mathbf{D}
implies that
F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p});k=\langle{id}_{F(X)},{id}_{F(X)}\rangle_{p,1-p}
.

Vice versa, if
F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p});k=\langle{id}_{F(X)},{id}_{F(X)}\rangle_{p,1-p}
then for every pair of arrows
f,g\in\mathbf{C}([X,Y])

\displaystyle F(f+_{p}g)=

\displaystyle F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p};(f\oplus g);\rhd_{Y})

(Eq.
6
)

\displaystyle=F(\langle{id}_{X},{id}_{X}\rangle_{p,1-p});k;(F(f)\oplus F(g));\rhd_{F(Y)}

(coproducts preserv.)

\displaystyle=\langle{id}_{F(X)},{id}_{F(X)}\rangle_{p,1-p};(F(f)\oplus F(g));\rhd_{F(Y)}

\displaystyle=F(f)+_{p}F(g).

(Eq.
6
)

Moreover,

\displaystyle F(\star_{X,Y})

\displaystyle=F(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Y})

(Lemma
7
.
1
)

\displaystyle=F(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{X});F(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Y})

\displaystyle=\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(X)};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(Y)}

(
0_{\mathbf{D}}
terminal +
F(0_{\mathbf{C}})\cong 0_{\mathbf{D}}
)

\displaystyle=\star_{F(X),F(Y)}.

(Lemma
7
.
1
)

Appendix 0.E
Appendix to Section
4

We break the proof of Proposition
3
in several intermediate results.

Lemma 11

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a PCA-enriched category.
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is a finite coproduct category.

Proof

We rely on Fox’s theorem
[
16
]
to prove this result. Recall the definitions of
\oplus
and
\sigma
in (
9
). Checking that
(\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C}),\oplus,0)
is a strict symmetric monoidal category amounts to several tedious matrix multiplications.
We proceed by illustrating that, for every object
P
,
(\rhd_{P},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P})
defined as in (
10
), is a natural and coherent comonoid.

Observe that, by the definitions in (
10
),
\rhd_{P}
where
P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
is the matrix

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
pNiceMatrix (nicematrix)

while
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}
is the unique
(n+1)\times 0
matrix.

Axioms in Table
1
follow by matrix multiplication. For instance, the associativity axiom
1
is obtained by the following computation:

({id}_{P}\oplus\rhd_{P});\rhd_{P}=\begin{pmatrix}{id}_{P}&{id}_{P}&{id}_{P}\end{pmatrix}=(\rhd_{P}\oplus{id}_{P});\rhd_{P}

and the naturality
1
by:

(M\oplus M);\rhd_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}M&M\end{pmatrix}=\rhd_{P};M

where
M\colon P\to Q
. Finally,

1
is obvious since the multiplication of an
m\times n
matrix with the
n\times 0
empty matrix is the empty
m\times 0
matrix.

We can collect the above observations and conclude that
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is a strict symmetric monoidal category in which every object
U
is equipped with a coherent and natural monoid structure
(U,\rhd_{U},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U})
, hence, by Fox’s theorem
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is a finite coproduct category.

Lemma 12

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a PCA-enriched category.
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is PCA-enriched.

Proof

Before proving the enrichment it is convenient to recall
(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P},\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P})
from (
10
) structure.
For
P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
,
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P}
is the
(2n\times n)
matrix illustrated below

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
pNiceMatrix (nicematrix)

while
\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}
is the empty
0\times(n+1)
matrix.
Axioms in Table
2
follow by matrix multiplication. For instance, in the case
U\in\mathbf{C}
, the associativity axiom
2
which states that
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(^{q}\!\!\lhd_{U}\oplus{id}_{U})=\,^{\tilde{p}}\!\!\lhd_{U};({id}_{U}\oplus^{\tilde{q}}\!\!\lhd_{U})
where
\tilde{p}=pq
and
\tilde{q}=\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq}

follows, for
U\in\mathbf{C}
, by the following computation

{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(^{q}\!\!\lhd_{U}\oplus{id}_{U})=\begin{pmatrix}q\cdot{id}_{U}&\emptyset\\
(1-q)\cdot{id}_{U}&\emptyset\\
\emptyset&1\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-p)\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}pq\cdot{id}_{U}\\
p(1-q)\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-p)\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{p}\cdot{id}_{U}\\
\tilde{q}(1-\tilde{p})\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-\tilde{p})(1-\tilde{p})\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}

which is equal to

{}^{\tilde{p}}\!\!\lhd_{U};({id}_{U}\oplus\,^{\tilde{q}}\!\!\lhd_{U})=\begin{pmatrix}1\cdot{id}_{U}&\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\tilde{q}\cdot{id}_{U}\\
\emptyset&(1-\tilde{q})\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{p}\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-\tilde{p})\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{p}\cdot{id}_{U}\\
\tilde{q}(1-\tilde{p})\cdot{id}_{U}\\
(1-\tilde{p})(1-\tilde{p})\cdot{id}_{U}\end{pmatrix}

The general case
P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
is obtained similarly. Axiom
2
is obvious since
p\cdot{id}_{U_{i}}+(1-p)\cdot{id}_{U_{i}}={id}_{U_{i}}
by PCA axiom
1
.
Axiom
2
is obtained as follows: for
M\colon U\to V

M;^{p}\!\!\lhd_{V}=\begin{pmatrix}p\cdot M\\
(1-p)\cdot M\end{pmatrix}M=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};M\oplus M

where
p\cdot M
denotes the matrix whose
(j,i)
-entries are given by
p\cdot M_{ji}
.
Finally,
2
is obvious since the multiplication of an
m\times n
matrix with the
n\times 0
empty matrix is the empty
m\times 0
matrix. Hence, we can collect the above observations and conclude that every object
U\in\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is equipped with a co-pca structure
(U,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U},\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U})
which satisfies the axioms in Table
2
.

Enrichment: the monoid and co-pca structure on every object of
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
induces a PCA-enrichment as follows. For every pair of objects
P
and
Q
and arrows
M,M^{\prime}\colon P\to Q
define

M+_{p}M^{\prime}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P};(M\oplus M^{\prime});\rhd_{Q}

matrix multiplication implies that

M+_{p}M^{\prime}_{ji}=(p\cdot M_{ji}+(1-p)\cdot M^{\prime}_{ji})=M_{ji}+_{p}M^{\prime}_{ji}

While
\star_{P,Q}
is given by
\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}
which is the
(m\times n)
matrix whose
(j,i)
-entry is given by
\star_{A_{i},B_{j}}
.

PCA-enrichment equations in (
3
) are obtained through axioms of coherent and natural monoid and co-pca structures, for instance, the first equation in (
3
)
N;(M+_{p}M^{\prime})=(N;M+_{p}N;M^{\prime})
, for
N\colon P^{\prime}\to P
, is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle N;(M+_{p}M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=N;(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P};(M\oplus M^{\prime});\rhd_{Q})

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P^{\prime}};(N\oplus N);(M\oplus M^{\prime});\rhd_{Q}

(axiom
2
)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P^{\prime}};(N;M\oplus N;M^{\prime});\rhd_{Q}

(monoidal structure)

\displaystyle=(N;M)+_{p}(N;M^{\prime})

Lemma 13

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a PCA-enriched category.
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
has convex binary products.

Proof

Let
M_{1}\colon P\to Q_{1}
and
M_{2}\colon P\to Q_{2}
be two morphisms in
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
and
p,q\in[0,1]
such that
p+q\leq 1
, where
P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
,
Q_{1}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m_{1}}V_{k}
and
Q_{2}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m_{2}}V^{\prime}_{k}
. Now consider
Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}
with

\pi_{1}\colon Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}\to Q_{1}
and
\pi_{2}\colon Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}\to Q_{2}
given by the
(m_{1}\times(m_{1}+m_{2}))
and
(m_{2}\times(m_{1}+m_{2}))
matrices

\pi_{1}=\begin{pNiceMatrix}

\pi_{2}=\begin{pNiceMatrix}

which correspond to
({id}_{Q_{1}}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q_{2}})
and
(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q_{1}}\oplus{id}_{Q_{2}})
respectively. Now, the arrow
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}
with
\vec{p}=p,q
given by the
(2n\times n)
matrix

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
pNiceMatrix (nicematrix)

matrix multiplication shows that

{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P};(M_{1}\oplus M_{2});\pi_{1}=p\cdot M_{1}\qquad^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P};(M_{1}\oplus M_{2});\pi_{2}=q\cdot M_{2}

Now, if
H\colon P\to Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
such that
H;\pi_{1}=p\cdot M_{1}
and
H;\pi_{2}=(1-p)\cdot M_{2}
, then

H_{ji}=p\cdot M_{ji}

for every
j=1,\dots,m_{1}
and
i=1,\dots,n
and

H_{ji}=q\cdot M^{\prime}_{(j-m_{1})i}

for every
j=m_{1}+1,\dots,m_{1}+m_{2}
and
i=1,\dots,n
. Hence,
H\equiv\,^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P};(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})
.
Observe that if
p=1
(and hence
q=0
) then
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}
is
\iota_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}({id}_{Q_{1}}\oplus\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q_{2}})
and if
p=0
(and hence
q=1
) then
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}
is
\iota_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q_{1}}\oplus{id}_{Q_{2}})
.

Proof
(Proof of Proposition
4
)

The functor
\mathbf{StMat}(F)
is strict monoidal and preserves monoids and co-pca objects, hence by Proposition
2
it is a morphism of convex biproduct categories.

Proof
(Proof of Theorem
4.1
)

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a PCA-enriched category and
\mathbf{D}
a convex biproduct category, and consider a PCA-enriched functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to U(\mathbf{D})
. The unit
\eta:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
is the identity on objects functor mapping an arrow
f\in\mathbf{C}[U,V]
into the
(1\times 1)
matrix with entry
1\cdot f
.

The functor
F^{\sharp}:\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
sends an object
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}
to
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}F(U_{k})
and an arrow
M\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}
, which corresponds to a matrix with entries
M_{ji}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}
, into the arrow of
\mathbf{D}
obtained as follows:
we first define the image of a column of the matrix, then we observe that
M
is the copairing of its columns, denoted with
M_{\bullet i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, and define
F^{\sharp}(M)
as the copairing of the images of the columns. The interpretation of the
i
-column
M_{\bullet i}
is defined as the arrow induced by the
m
-ary convex product through the vector
\vec{p}=(p_{1i},\dots,p_{mi})
and the arrows
F(f_{ji})
in
\mathbf{D}
, which exists by Lemma
2
.
F^{\sharp}
is well defined since, for
M\equiv M^{\prime}
, it holds that
M_{ji}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}=p^{\prime}_{ji}\cdot f^{\prime}_{ji}=M^{\prime}_{ji}
. Now, for
k=1,\dots m

\displaystyle F^{\sharp}(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i});\pi_{k}

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}_{ki}\cdot F(f^{\prime}_{ki})

(Def.
F^{\sharp}(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i})
)

\displaystyle=F(p^{\prime}_{ki}\cdot f^{\prime}_{ki})

(
F
PCA-enriched)

\displaystyle=F(p_{ki}\cdot f_{ki})

(
M\cong M^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=p_{ki}\cdot F(f_{ki})

(
F
PCA-enriched)

and the universal property of convex products in
\mathbf{D}
implies that
F^{\sharp}(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i})=F^{\sharp}(M_{\bullet i})
and, hence,
F^{\sharp}(M^{\prime})=F^{\sharp}(M)
by the universal property of coproducts in
\mathbf{D}
. Moreover, a simple computation shows that for every object
U\in\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})
,
F^{\sharp}
preserves monoids and co-pca structures, i.e. it holds:

•

F^{\sharp}(\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U})=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{F(U)}
and
F^{\sharp}(\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U})=\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{F(U)}
;

•

F^{\sharp}(\,\rhd_{U})=\rhd_{F(U)}
and
F^{\sharp}(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{F(U)}
;

•

F^{\sharp}(1\cdot f)=F(f)
, for every
f\colon U\to V
in
\mathbf{C}
;

Hence, by Proposition
2
it follows that
F^{\sharp}
is a morphism of convex biproduct categories. It is also the unique morphism such that
\eta;F^{\sharp}=F
since any other morphism
G:\mathbf{StMat}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
such that
\eta;G=F
must preserve coproducts and convex products by Proposition
9
and
4
. Hence,
F^{\sharp}=G
since every column of
M
is the arrow induced by the m-ary convex product through
\vec{p}=(p_{1i},\dots,p_{mi})
and the arrows
f_{ji}
for a fixed
i
.

Proof
(Proof of Proposition
5
)

Recall that the counit
\epsilon
is obtained as
{id}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\sharp}
, following the notation in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Fullness follows from the fact that every arrow
f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{C}
if equal to
\epsilon(\eta(f))
by construction. Faithfulness is obtained by observing that if
\epsilon(M)=\epsilon(M^{\prime})
for two matrices in
\mathbf{StMat}(U(\mathbf{C}))
,
M,M^{\prime}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}U_{k}\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}
with entries
M_{ji}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}
and
M^{\prime}_{ji}=p^{\prime}_{ji}\cdot f^{\prime}_{ji}
, then the copairing of the image through
\epsilon
of the columns of
M
is equal to the copairing of the image through
\epsilon
of the columns of
M^{\prime}
. The universal property of the coproducts in
\mathbf{C}
then ensures that for every
i
-column of
M
and
M^{\prime}
, denoted with
M_{\bullet i}
and
M^{\prime}_{\bullet i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
, it holds
\epsilon(M_{\bullet i})=\epsilon(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i})
. Now, since
\epsilon(M_{\bullet i})
is given by the arrow induced by the m-ary convex product through
f_{1i},\dots,f_{mi}
, with
\vec{p}=(p_{1i},\dots,p_{mi})
, and
\epsilon(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i})
is given by the arrow induced by the m-ary convex product through
f^{\prime}_{1i},\dots,f^{\prime}_{mi}
, with
\vec{p}^{\prime}=(p^{\prime}_{1i},\dots,p^{\prime}_{mi})
, it follows that
\epsilon(M_{\bullet i});\pi_{j}=p_{ji}\cdot f_{ji}=p^{\prime}_{ji}\cdot f^{\prime}_{ji}=\epsilon(M^{\prime}_{\bullet i})\pi_{j}
, for
j=1,\dots,m
. Hence,
M_{ji}\equiv M^{\prime}_{ji}
and therefore they are equal in
\mathbf{StMat}(U(\mathbf{C}))
.

Proof
(Proof of Theorem

)

The fact that the definitions of

and

in (

) provides a

2

2
. The fact that this provides an enrichment of

,

\multimapinv

and

see, e.g.,

]
.

The fact that

and

).

Since, by the axioms in Figure

, every object

is equipped with a natural and coherent monoid, by
[

]
, it holds that
\oplus
is a coproducts with injections

\iota_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{P_{1}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P_{1}}\oplus{id}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{2}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\text{.}

Recall that (see for instance
[
35
, Ch. 6.4]
) the copairing of
\mathfrak{t}\colon P_{1}\to Q
and
\mathfrak{s}\colon P_{2}\to Q
is defined as

[\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(f\oplus g);\rhd_{Q}

and thus
[{id}_{Q},{id}_{Q}]=\rhd_{Q}
.

Our main technical effort, illustrated below in Proposition
11
, consists in proving that

is also a convex product with projections

\pi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{P_{1}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P_{1}}\oplus{id}_{P_{2}}\colon P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{2}\text{.}

and the convex pairing of
\mathfrak{t}\colon P\to Q_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}\colon P\to Q_{2}
is given as

<\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}>_{1,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{t}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q_{2}}\qquad<\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}>_{0,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q_{1}}\oplus\mathfrak{s}\qquad<\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}>_{p,1-p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\;^{p}\!\!\lhd_{P};(\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{s})\text{ for }p\in(0,1)

As we will illustrate later, the case of
<\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}>_{p,q}
with
p+q<1
simply follows from the above one.

Before delving in proof of Proposition
11
observe that (
5
) in Definition
2
follows easily by the above definitions of

and

, axioms (

), for the case

.
Instead the laws (

and

and the definition of

in (

).

Finally, to prove that
\langle\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots\mathfrak{t}_{n}\rangle_{\vec{p}}=^{\;\;\vec{p}}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i}
, one can easily check that, for all
i
,
(^{\;\;\vec{p}}\!\!\!\lhd_{U}^{n};\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i});\pi_{i}=p_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{i}
, where the
i

is defined as

.

The proof of Proposition
11
substantially relies on a normal form result (Lemma
21
) and on the fact that the enrichment on
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
is cancellative, namely if

, then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{s}
(Lemma

). The remainder of this appendix is structured as follows: we first illustrate some preliminary results, then some results about normal forms, then the cancellativity property and finally convex products.

Let
\mathfrak{t}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
and
\mathfrak{s}=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2})
. If
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\frac{q}{p}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\frac{1-p}{1-q}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{s}
.

Proof

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd;(\frac{q}{p}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(Lemma
16
.1)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd;(^{\frac{q}{p}\,\,}\!\!\!\lhd\oplus{id});(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(Lemma
14
)

\displaystyle=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd_{;}({id}\oplus\,^{\frac{p-q}{1-q}\,\,}\!\!\!\lhd);(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(
2
)

\displaystyle=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd_{;}({id}\oplus(^{\frac{1-p}{1-q}\,\,}\!\!\!\lhd;\sigma));(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}))

(
2
)

\displaystyle=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd_{;}({id}\oplus(^{\frac{1-p}{1-q}\,\,}\!\!\!\lhd));(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})

(Symmetric Monoidal Category)

\displaystyle=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\frac{1-p}{1-q}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(Lemma
16
.2)

\displaystyle=s

(Hypothesis)

0.F.2
Normal forms

We are now going to prove several normal form results, that will be crucial in the proof of Proposition
11
.
For these results we need to fix some notation.

For all natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in(0,1)
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\leq 1
one can define
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}\colon P\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n+1}P
as follows:

(n=0):\ ^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}{id}_{U}\qquad(n=k+1):\ ^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\,^{p_{1}}\!\!\lhd_{P};({id}_{P}\oplus\,^{\frac{\vec{p^{\prime}}}{1-p_{1}}}\!\!\lhd_{P})

(21)

where
\vec{p^{\prime}}=p_{2},\dots,p_{n}
.

Observe that
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{P}
differs from the definition of
{}^{\vec{p}\;}\!\!\!\lhd_{P}^{n}
given in (
14
), but we need this formulation for the rest of the section.

Similarly, for all natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
one can define
\rhd_{U}^{n}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}P\to P
as follows:

(n=0):\ \rhd_{P}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{P}\qquad(n+1):\ \rhd_{P}^{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}({id}_{P}\oplus\rhd_{P}^{n});\rhd_{P}.

(22)

Lemma 18
(Pre normal form)

Every arrow
\mathfrak{t}\colon\oplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to\oplus_{i=1}^{m}V_{i}
in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
can be written as
\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2};\mathfrak{t}_{3};\mathfrak{t}_{4}
where:

•

\mathfrak{t}_{1}
is obtained through the fragment

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{0}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}

•

\mathfrak{t}_{2}
is obtained through the fragment

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{0}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}

•

\mathfrak{t}_{3}
is obtained through the fragment

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{0}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}

•

\mathfrak{t}_{4}
is obtained through the fragment

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\rhd_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{0}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

{id}_{U}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t};\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{t}

\!\!\!\mid\!\!\!

\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}

Proof

Using the naturality, one can first move all the occurrences of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
to the left. The all the occurrences of

\multimap

to the left until an occurence of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
is met (note that since

\multimap

is not the unit of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
,

\multimap

remains at the right of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd
). Similarly, one can move all occurrences of
\rhd
and

\multimap

on the right.

Lemma 19

Let
\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to V
be an arrow of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
. Then one of the following holds:

•

\mathfrak{t}=\star_{A,B}
;

•

there exist
c\in\mathbf{C}[U,V]
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}
;

•

there exist distinct
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbf{C}[U,V]
and
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n+1}
such that

\mathfrak{t}=\,^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{A};((\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c_{i}\vphantom{t}\;}}})\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U});\rhd_{V}^{n}\text{.}

Proof

By Lemma
18
,
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2};\mathfrak{t}_{3};\mathfrak{t}_{4}
.
Using the naturality of
\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}
(see Table
3
), one can move all the occurrences of
\sigma_{U,V}^{\oplus}
to the right until an occurrence of
\rhd_{V}
is met.
Using the associativity and the symmetry of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}
all multiple occurrences of every
c_{i}
and
\star_{U,V}
can be collected in the form
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(c_{i}\otimes c_{i})
and then collapsed to one occurrence of
c_{i}
by using the idempotency axiom and axioms for
\rhd_{U}
. Then again using the associativity and the symmetry of
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U}
one can move all the occurrences of in the form of
{}^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{U}
for some vector
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
, and similarly all occurrences of
\rhd_{V}
in the form of
\rhd_{V}^{n}
.

Lemma 20

Arrows in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
of the form
\mathfrak{t}:U\to V
are in bijection with
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{C}[U,V])
.

Proof

By Lemma
19
every arrow
f:U\to V
is in one of the following forms:

•

\mathfrak{t}=\star_{U,V}
;

•

there exists
c\in\mathbf{C}[U,V]
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c\vphantom{t}\;}}}
;

•

there exist distinct
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbf{C}[U,V]
and
\vec{p}=p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
such that

\mathfrak{t}=\,^{\vec{p}}\!\!\lhd_{U};((\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c_{i}\vphantom{t}\;}}})\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U});\rhd_{V}^{n}\text{.}

The first two cases correspond respectively to the zero subdistribution and the Dirac distribution on
c
. The third case corresponds to the subdistribution
\vec{p}
on the set of arrows
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\}
, where
p_{i}
is the probability of
c_{i}
. If
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\,^{\vec{q}}\!\!\lhd_{U};((\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\underline{\smash{\overline{\;c^{\prime}_{i}\vphantom{t}\;}}})\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U});\rhd_{V}^{n^{\prime}}
are sent to the same subdistribution, then one has that
n=n^{\prime}
,
p_{i}=q_{i}
and
c_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Lemma 21

Let
\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
be an arrow of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
. Then,

•

either
\mathfrak{t}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}}
,

•

or there exists
j\in 1\dots n
and
\mathfrak{t}_{j}\colon U\to V_{j}
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j-1}V_{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{j}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}}
,

•

or for all
j\in 1\dots n
, there exists
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}V_{i}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{;}(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2})
.

Proof

We have that
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2};\mathfrak{t}_{3};\mathfrak{t}_{4}
where the
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
are prescribed as by Lemma
18
.
We consider two different cases: either
\mathfrak{t}_{1}={id}_{U}
or
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\neq{id}_{U}
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{t}_{1}={id}_{U}\colon U\to U
. Then
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
is an arrow with domain
U
and thus either
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}
or
\mathfrak{t}_{2}={id}_{U}
.

•

If
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\colon U\to 0
, then
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
is an arrow with domain
0
. This entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
should be
{id}_{0}\colon 0\to 0
and
\mathfrak{t}_{4}
and arrow of type
0\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
. The latter fact entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{4}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}}
. Thus
\mathfrak{t}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}}
.

•

If
\mathfrak{t}_{2}={id}_{U}\colon U\to U
, then
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
should have type
U\to V_{j}
and thus it must be either
{id}_{U}
or
\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}
. We proceed with the case for
\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}
, the one for
{id}_{U}
is identical. Since
\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}\colon U\to V_{j}
then
\mathfrak{t}_{4}
should have type
V_{j}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
. This entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{4}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j-1}V_{i}}\oplus{id}_{V_{j}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}}
. Thus
\mathfrak{t}=\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j-1}V_{i}}\oplus{id}_{V_{j}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}}
which, by the laws of symmetric monoidal categories, is equal to
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j-1}V_{i}}\oplus\overline{\underline{\;c\vphantom{d_{A}}\;}}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}}
. Thus
\mathfrak{t}
is in the second shape.

Let us consider now the case
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\neq{id}_{U}
. For any
j\in 1\dots n
,
\mathfrak{t}_{4}
can be written as
\rhd_{P}^{x}\oplus\rhd_{Q}^{y}
where

x,y\in\mathbb{N}
,
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}V_{i}
and
Q=\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{i}

and
\rhd_{P}^{x}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}P\to P
and
\rhd_{Q}^{y}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}Q\to Q
are the arrows defined in (
22
).

Now
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
should be have as codomain
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}P\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}Q
. Since
\mathfrak{t}
has domain
U
,
\mathfrak{t}_{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2}
has type
U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{z}U
for some
z\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the arrows in
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
preserve the size of domain and codomains we have that, the domain of
\mathfrak{t}_{3}
can be written as
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}U)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}U)
. This fact entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{3}=\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{2}
for some
\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{1}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}U)\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}V_{j})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{2}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}U)\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}V_{n})
.

Now
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
should be have as codomain
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}U)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}U)
. Since the arrows in the shape of
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
can only decrease the size of codomain,
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
has domain
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{x^{\prime}}U)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{y^{\prime}}U)
for some
x^{\prime}\geq j
and
y^{\prime}\geq n-j+1
.
This fact entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{2}
for some
\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{1}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{x^{\prime}}U)\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{j}U)
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{2}\colon\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{y^{\prime}}U)\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=j+1}^{n}U)
.

Now
\mathfrak{t}_{1}
has type
U\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{x^{\prime}}U)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{y^{\prime}}U)
. Note that the latter object is different from
U
as
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\neq{id}_{U}
. By using (
2
) and (
2
),
\mathfrak{t}_{1}
can be written in the form
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{2})
for some
\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{1}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{x}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{x^{\prime}}U)
and
\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{2}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{k=1}^{y}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{y^{\prime}}U)
.

In summary,
\mathfrak{t}=^{p}\!\!\lhd;(\,(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{1};\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{1};\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{1};\rhd_{P}^{x})\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{2};\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{2};\mathfrak{t}_{3}^{2};\rhd_{Q}^{y})\,)
is in the third form.

Proof
(Proof of Lemma
3
)

By iteratively applying Lemma
21
.

0.F.3
Cancellativity

In order to prove cancellativity, we first consider the case of arrows of type
U\to V
where
U,V
are objects of
\mathbf{C}
.

Lemma 22

For all
r\in(0,1)
, for all
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to V
,
if
r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}
then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Proof

By Lemma
20
, we known
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})[U,V]=\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(\mathbf{C}[U,V])
. It is well known (see e.g.
[
43
]
) that, for all sets
X
,
\mathcal{D}_{\leq}(X)
is a cancellative pca.

Then, we consider the case of arrows of type
U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
where
U,V_{i}
are objects of
\mathbf{C}
.

Lemma 23

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
r\in(0,1)
, for all
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
,
if
r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}
then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Proof

We proceed by induction on
n
.

The base case
n=0
, it is trivial since
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
is the final object
0
.

For the induction case
n+1
, we exploit Lemma
21
and we consider the case where

\mathfrak{s}=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd;(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(23)

for
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon U\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2},\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon U\to V_{n+1}
. The other cases are trivial.

By Lemma
16
.3, we have that

r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=\,^{q}\!\!\lhd;(r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad r\cdot\mathfrak{t}=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd;(r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(24)

Since by hypothesis
r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}
, it holds both

r\cdot\mathfrak{s};({id}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})=r\cdot\mathfrak{t};({id}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad r\cdot\mathfrak{s};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}\oplus{id})=r\cdot\mathfrak{t};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}\oplus{id})\text{.}

Thus, by (
24
) and Lemma
16
.1 and 2, one obtains that

q\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}=p\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(1-q)\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}=(1-p)\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}\text{.}

(25)

There are now three cases:
p=q
,
p>q
and
p<q
.

If
p=q
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1}
by induction hypothesis and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}
by Lemma
22
. Then, by (
23
),
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

If
p>q
, we can rewrite (
25
) as

p\cdot r\cdot\frac{q}{p}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}=p\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(1-q)\cdot r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}=(1-q)\cdot r\cdot\frac{1-p}{1-q}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}\text{.}

Thus, by induction hypothesis
\frac{q}{p}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1}
and, by Lemma
22
,
\mathfrak{s}_{2}=\frac{1-p}{1-q}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}
. By (
23
) and Lemma
17
, we have that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.
The case
p<q
is symmetrical

We can finally consider the general case.

Proof
(Proof of Lemma
4
)

Let
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
. We proceed by induction on
n
. The base case
n=0
, it is trivial since
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
is the initial object
0
.

For the induction case
n+1
, since
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has finite coproducts, one can assume without loss of generality that

\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2});\rhd_{Q}\qquad\text{ and }\mathfrak{t}=(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});\rhd_{Q}

(26)

for some
\mathfrak{s}_{2},\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon U_{n+1}\to Q
and
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon P^{\prime}\to Q
where
P^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
. Note moreover that the coproduct universal property entails that

\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}\text{ iff }\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1}\text{ and }\mathfrak{s}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}\text{.}

(27)

By Lemma
15
, we have that

r\cdot\mathfrak{s}=(r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1})\oplus(r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2});\rhd_{Q}
and
r\cdot\mathfrak{t}=(r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1})\oplus(r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2});\rhd_{Q}
. Thus by (
27
) we have that

r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad r\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}=r\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}

From the rightmost above and Lemma
23
, we derive that
\mathfrak{s}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}
. From the leftmost above and induction hypothesis, we derive that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1}
. By (
27
), it holds that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{t}
.

0.F.4
Convex Products

Now, proving
<\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}>_{p,1-p}
is the unique mediating morphism is pretty easy in the case
p\in\{0,1\}
. It follows immediately by the following results.

Lemma 24

Let
\mathfrak{t}\colon U\to Q\oplus R
be an arrow in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
.

1.

If
\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}

and
\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{s}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
;

2.

If
\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}

and
\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}
then
\mathfrak{t}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}\oplus\mathfrak{s}
;

Proof

We prove the first point: the second is symmetrical.

First, observe that
\mathfrak{t}
should be in one of the form of Lemma
21
. The only challenging case is the third form (iii)
\mathfrak{t}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
for some
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon U\to Q
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}:U\to R
. Since

\displaystyle\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})

(Hypothesis)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})

(iii)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};((\mathfrak{t}_{1};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(Symmetric Monoidal Category)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(
2
)

\displaystyle=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}

(Lemma
16
.2)

since also
(1-p)\cdot(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
, by cancellativity (Lemma
4
), we have that
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R}))\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(hypothesis)

\displaystyle=(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R}))\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(iii)

\displaystyle=(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{2};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})));\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(Symmetric Monoidal Category)

\displaystyle=(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})));\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
)

\displaystyle=(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U}))\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(
2
)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}))

(Symmetric Monoidal Category)

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})

(
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{t}

(iii)

The lemma above is generalised to arbitrary arrows as follows. The proof substantially relies on the fact that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has coproducts.

Lemma 25

Let
\mathfrak{t}\colon P\to Q\oplus R
be an arrow in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
where
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
.

1.

If
\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}

and
\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{s}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
;

2.

If
\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}

and
\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}
then
\mathfrak{t}=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}\oplus\mathfrak{s}
;

Proof

The proof proceeds by induction on
n
. The base case
n=0
is trivial since
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{0}U_{i}
is the initial object
0
.

For the induction case
n+1
, since
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has finite coproducts, we can assume that
\mathfrak{t}=(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});\rhd_{Q\oplus R}
where
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to Q\oplus R

and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon U_{n+1}\to Q\oplus R
and
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2});\rhd_{Q}
where
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to Q
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}\colon U_{n+1}\to Q
.

Now, if
\mathfrak{t};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2};\rhd_{Q\oplus R};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})\oplus({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R});\rhd_{Q}

(axiom
1
)

and hence
\mathfrak{t}_{1};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2};({id}_{Q}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{R})=\mathfrak{s}_{2}
. Similarly, from
\mathfrak{t};(\,\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
, using the axiom
1
and
2
, we have that
\mathfrak{t}_{1};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{Q}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}\oplus{id}_{R})=\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U_{n+1}};\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
. Hence, by inductive hypothesis, we have that
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
and, from Lemma
24
,
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{s}_{2}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}
. Now, the statement is obtained as follows

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2};\rhd_{Q\oplus R}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R})\oplus(\mathfrak{s}_{2}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R});\rhd_{Q\oplus R}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R})\oplus(\mathfrak{s}_{2}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R});({id}_{Q}\oplus\sigma_{R,Q}\oplus{id}_{R});\rhd_{Q}\oplus\rhd_{R}

(axiom
1
)

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2});\rhd_{Q}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

(axiom
1
)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimap$}_{R}

Proving that
<\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{t}_{2}>_{p,1-p}
is the convex pairing in the case of
p\in(0,1)
relies on cancellativity.
We first prove the case for
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon U\to P
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon U\to Q
where
U
is an object of
\mathbf{C}

Lemma 26

Let
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon U\to P
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon U\to Q
be arrows of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
and
p\in(0,1)
. Then
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})\colon U\to P\oplus Q
is the unique arrow
\mathfrak{t}
such that

p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})
and
(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})
.

Proof

First observe that by Lemma
16
.1 and 2 we have that
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}
and that
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2});(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}
.

We now need to prove that
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
is the unique arrow with such property. We prove that if (a)
p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})
and (b)
(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})
then
\mathfrak{t}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
.

Note that
\mathfrak{t}
can be in one of the forms of Lemma
21
. The only relevant case is the last one:
\mathfrak{t}=\,^{P^{\prime}}\!\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime})
. By (a), (b) Lemma
16
.1 and 2, it holds that

p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}=p^{\prime}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}
and
(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=(1-p^{\prime})\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}
.

There are now three cases:

•

If
p=p^{\prime}
, then by Lemma
4
,
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{t}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
.

•

If
p>p^{\prime}
, then we can rewrite the above equalities as
p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}=p\cdot\frac{p^{\prime}}{p}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}
and
(1-p^{\prime})\frac{(1-p)}{1-p^{\prime}}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=(1-p^{\prime})\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus by Lemma
4
, we have that
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\frac{p^{\prime}}{p}\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\frac{1-p}{1-p^{\prime}}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus, by Lemma
17
,
\mathfrak{t}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U};(\mathfrak{t}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2})
.

•

The case
p<p^{\prime}
is symmetrical to the one above.

The lemma above is generalised to arbitrary arrows as follows. The proof substantially relies on the fact that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has coproducts.

Lemma 27

Let
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to P
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to Q
be arrows of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
and
p\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a unique arrow
\mathfrak{t}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to P\oplus Q
such that

p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}=\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})
and
(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}=\mathfrak{t};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})
.

Proof

The proof proceed by induction on
n
. The case
n=0
is trivial since
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{0}U_{i}
is the initial object
0
.

We consider the case
n+1
. Since
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has coproducts then

\mathfrak{t}_{1}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2});\rhd_{P}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t}_{2}=(\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2});\rhd_{Q}

(28)

for
\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to P
,
\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\colon U_{n+1}\to P
,
\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{1}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to Q
,
\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}\colon U_{n+1}\to Q
.

By induction hypothesis there exists a unique arrow
\mathfrak{t}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to P\oplus Q
such that

\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{1}

(29)

We now focus on
\mathfrak{t}\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}));\rhd_{P\oplus Q}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n+1}U_{i}\to P\oplus Q
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}));\rhd_{P\oplus Q};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})

\displaystyle=(\,(\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2});({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

(nat. of
\rhd_{P\oplus Q}
)

\displaystyle=(\,(\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{U_{n+1}}))\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

(nat. of

\multimapinv

)

\displaystyle=(\,(\mathfrak{t};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\oplus p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

(Lemma
14
)

\displaystyle=(\,p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\oplus p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

(
29
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot(\,(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2});\rhd_{P\oplus Q}\,)

(Lemma
15
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}

(
28
)

Similar computations proves that
\mathfrak{t}\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}));\rhd_{P\oplus Q};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}
.

Now we need to prove that
\mathfrak{t}\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}));\rhd_{P\oplus Q}
is the unique arrows with such property.
Assume that
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n+1}U_{i}\to P\oplus Q
is such that

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t}^{\prime};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}

(30)

Since
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has coproducts
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime});\rhd_{P\oplus Q}
for some
\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\to P\oplus Q
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}\colon U_{n+1}\to P\oplus Q
.
By using naturality of
\rhd_{P\oplus Q}
one readily has

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=(\,(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}\text{.}

(31)

Now, it holds that

\displaystyle(\,(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q}))\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})

(
31
)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}

(
30
)

\displaystyle=(\,p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\oplus p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\,);\rhd_{P\oplus Q}

(derivation above)

and thus, by the universal property of coproducts, we can conclude that

\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime};({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\text{.}

(32)

With the symmetric argument, one obtains that

\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime};(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}\text{.}

(33)

Now, by the leftmost equations in (
32
) and (
33
) and the fact that
\mathfrak{t}
is the unique satisfying (
29
), we obtain that
\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime}=t
.

By Lemma
26
,
{}^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2})
is the unique arrow
h\colon U_{n+1}\to P\oplus Q
such that
h;({id}_{P}\oplus\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{Q})=p\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}
and
h;(\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$}_{P}\oplus{id}_{Q})=(1-p)\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}
. Thus, the rightmost equations in (
32
) and (
33
) entails that
\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\prime}=^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2})
. This proves that
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus(^{p}\!\!\lhd_{U_{n+1}};(\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{s}_{2}^{2}));\rhd_{P\oplus Q}
.

We can finally state our main result.

Proposition 11

\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
has convex products.

Proof

We need to prove that for all arrows
\mathfrak{t}_{1}\colon R\to P
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}\colon R\to Q
and for all
p,q\in[0,1]
such that
p+q\leq 1
, there exists a unique
\mathfrak{t}\colon R\to P\oplus Q
such that
\mathfrak{t};\pi_{1}=p\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{t};\pi_{2}=q\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{2}
.

The case when either
p=1
or
q=1
is proved by Lemma
25
.
The case when
p,q\in(0,1)
and
p+q=1
is proved by Lemma
27
.
The case when
p,q\in(0,1)
and
p+q<1
easily follows from the previous case: if
p\geq 1-q
one can take
\langle f,g\rangle_{p,q}=\langle f,\frac{q}{1-p}\cdot g\rangle_{p,1-p}
.
If
p\leq 1-q
one can take
\langle f,g\rangle_{p,q}=\langle\frac{p}{1-q}\cdot f,g\rangle_{1-q,q}
.

0.F.5
Proof of Proposition
6

We conclude this appendix with a proof of Proposition
6
that turns out to be useful when considering diagrammatic languages (see Section
7
). First, we need the following result.

Proposition 12

Given a category
\mathbf{C}
and a congruence relation
\sim
on
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
, then the functor
Q_{\sim}\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
satisfies the following property: if
[p_{1}\cdot f_{1}]_{\sim}=[q_{1}\cdot g_{1}]_{\sim}
and
[p_{2}\cdot f_{2}]_{\sim}=[q_{2}\cdot g_{2}]_{\sim}
then
[\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}]_{\sim}=[\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(q_{1},q_{2})}]_{\sim}
.

Proof

Before proceeding with the proof, we make the trivial observation that if
[f]_{\sim}=[g]_{\sim}
then
[p\cdot f]_{\sim}=[p\cdot g]_{\sim}
for all
p\in[0,1]
. Indeed,

\displaystyle p\cdot f

\displaystyle=\,^{p}\!\!\!\lhd;(f\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})

(Thm.
5.1
)

\displaystyle\sim\,^{p}\!\!\!\lhd;(g\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})

(by
[f]_{\sim}=[g]_{\sim}
)

\displaystyle=\,p\cdot g

hence
[p\cdot f]_{\sim}=[p\cdot g]_{\sim}
.
The cases with
p=0
and
p=1
are trivial.
Now, define
r_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}f_{i}&\text{if }p_{i}\leq q_{i}\\
g_{i}&\text{else}\end{cases}
,
u_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}p_{i}&\text{if }p_{i}\leq q_{i}\\
q_{i}&\text{else}\end{cases}
and consider
\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle_{(u_{1},u_{2})}
. We prove that
[\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}]_{\sim}=[\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle_{(u_{1},u_{2})}]_{\sim}=[\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(q_{1},q_{2})}]_{\sim}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
p_{1}\leq q_{1}
and
q_{2}\leq p_{2}
, hence
\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle_{(u_{1},u_{2})}=\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},q_{2})}
. The first equality is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}

\displaystyle=\,^{p_{1}}\!\!\!\lhd;(f_{1}\oplus\,(^{\frac{p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(f_{2}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(Thm.
5.1
)

\displaystyle\sim\,^{p_{1}}\!\!\!\lhd;(f_{1}\oplus\,(^{\frac{q_{2}}{1-p_{1}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{2}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(by
[p_{2}\cdot f_{2}]_{\sim}=[q_{2}\cdot g_{2}]_{\sim}
)

\displaystyle=\,\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},q_{2})}

hence
[\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}]_{\sim}=[\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},q_{2})}]_{\sim}
. The second equality is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(q_{1},q_{2})}

\displaystyle=\,^{q_{1}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{1}\oplus\,(^{\frac{q_{2}}{1-q_{1}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{2}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(Thm.
5.1
)

\displaystyle=\,^{q_{2}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{2}\oplus\,(^{\frac{q_{1}}{1-q_{2}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{1}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(
2
+
2
)

\displaystyle\sim\,^{q_{2}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{2}\oplus\,(^{\frac{p_{1}}{1-q_{2}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(f_{1}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(by
[p_{1}\cdot f_{1}]_{\sim}=[q_{1}\cdot g_{1}]_{\sim}
)

\displaystyle=^{p_{1}}\!\!\!\lhd;(f_{1}\oplus\,(^{\frac{q_{2}}{1-p_{1}}}\!\!\!\lhd;(g_{2}\oplus\,\!\rotatebox{90.0}{$\multimapinv$})))

(
2
+
2
)

\displaystyle=\,\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},q_{2})}

hence
[\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(q_{1},q_{2})}]_{\sim}=[\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{(p_{1},q_{2})}]_{\sim}
.

Proof
(Proof of Proposition
6
)

Since
\sim
is a congruence relation,
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
is a monoidal category and
Q_{\sim}
is a monoidal functor. The object
0
is initial and terminal in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
since it is so in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
. The diagram
A\overset{[\iota_{1}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim}}{\rightarrow}A\oplus B\overset{[\iota_{2}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim}}{\leftarrow}B
is the coproduct of
A
and
B
since for every pair of arrows
[f]_{\sim}\colon A\to X
and
[g]_{\sim}:B\to X
there exists the arrow
[(f\oplus g);\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}
that gives
[\iota_{1}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[(f\oplus g);\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}=[f]_{\sim}
and
[\iota_{B}]_{\sim};[(f\oplus g);\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}=[g]_{\sim}
. Such arrow is the unique satisfying this property, indeed if
[h]\colon A\oplus B\to X
satisfies
[\iota_{1}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[h]_{\sim}=[f]_{\sim}
and
[\iota_{2}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[h]_{\sim}=[g]_{\sim}
, then coproducts in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
imply that there exist
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
such that
h\coloneqq[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}];\rhd_{X}
and then

\displaystyle[\iota_{1}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[h]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[\iota_{1}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}];\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}

(
h\coloneqq[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}];\rhd_{X}
)

\displaystyle=[f^{\prime}]_{\sim}

(

congruence relation)

\displaystyle=[f]_{\sim}

(by hp.)

similarly
[\iota_{2}^{A\oplus B}]_{\sim};[h]_{\sim}=[g^{\prime}]_{\sim}=[g]_{\sim}
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle[h]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}];\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}

\displaystyle=[f^{\prime}]_{\sim}\oplus[g^{\prime}]_{\sim};[\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}

(
\sim
congruence relation)

\displaystyle=[f]_{\sim}\oplus[g]_{\sim};[\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}

(above equalities)

\displaystyle=[(f\oplus g);\rhd_{X}]_{\sim}

(

congruence relation)

\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})_{\sim}
is also enriched over convex algebras. Indeed, for all

, one defines

for all

and
p\in[0,1]
. This is well defined since if

and

then

\displaystyle=[^{p}\!\!\!\lhd_{X};(f\oplus g);\rhd_{Y}]_{\sim}

)

(

congruence relation)

\displaystyle=[^{p}\!\!\!\lhd_{X}]_{\sim};([f^{\prime}]_{\sim}\oplus[g^{\prime}]_{\sim});[\rhd_{Y}]_{\sim}

(by

and

)

\displaystyle=[f^{\prime}+_{p}g^{\prime}]_{\sim}

While
\star_{X,Y}
is just
[\star_{X,Y}]_{\sim}
. Axioms of PCA are satisfied since they are so in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
and
\sim
is a congruence relation. Now we prove that

is a binary convex product also in
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{C})
. Indeed, given
[f]_{\sim}\colon X\to A
and
[g]_{\sim}\colon X\to B
,
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
p_{1}+p_{2}\leq 1
, we prove that there is a unique
[h]_{\sim}\colon X\to A\oplus B
such that
[h]_{\sim};[\pi_{1}]_{\sim}=p_{1}\cdot[f]_{\sim}
and
[h]_{\sim};[\pi_{2}]_{\sim}=p_{2}\cdot[g]_{\sim}
. Existence is provided by
h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\langle f,g\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}
, for uniqueness, suppose that there is also
[h^{\prime}]_{\sim}
with the same property. Then, by Theorem
5.1
,
h^{\prime}=\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
for some
f^{\prime}\colon X\to A
,
g^{\prime}\colon X\to B
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
q_{1}+q_{2}\leq 1
. Now, since
[h^{\prime}]_{\sim};[\pi_{1}]_{\sim}=[q_{1}\cdot f^{\prime}]_{\sim}=[p_{1}\cdot f]_{\sim}
and
[h^{\prime}]_{\sim};[\pi_{2}]_{\sim}=[q_{2}\cdot g^{\prime}]_{\sim}=p_{2}\cdot[g]_{\sim}
, by Proposition
12
we have that
[h^{\prime}]_{\sim}=[\langle f,g\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}]_{\sim}
. Hence,
\langle[f],[g]\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}\coloneqq[\langle f,g\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2})}]
is the unique arrow with the required property. Observe also that, by definition,
[f]_{\sim}+_{p}[g]_{\sim}=[^{p}\!\!\lhd];([f]_{\sim}\oplus[g]_{\sim});[\rhd]_{\sim}
. The fact that
Q_{\sim}
is a convex biproduct functor because is PCA-enriched and preserves coproducts.

By induction on

we prove that

for all
